A fast algorithm for the Frobenius problem in three variables
Daniel Rosin

Abstract
Given the function Γ ⁡ ( X ) = a ​ 1 ​ x ​ 1 + a ​ 2 ​ x ​ 2 + a ​ 3 ​ x ​ 3 \Gamma(X)=a\textsubscript{1}x\textsubscript{1}+a\textsubscript{2}x\textsubscript{2}+a\textsubscript{3}x\textsubscript{3} , where the set { a ​ 1 , a ​ 2 , a ​ 3 } \{a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3}\} , denoted A A , consists of positive integers and the set { x ​ 1 , x ​ 2 , x ​ 3 } \{x\textsubscript{1},x\textsubscript{2},x\textsubscript{3}\} , denoted X X , consists of non-negative integers, the Frobenius problem in three variables is to find the greatest integer, which is not in the codomain of Γ \Gamma . The fastest known algorithm for solving the three variable case of the Frobenius problem was invented by H. Greenberg in 1988 whose worst case time complexity is a logarithmic function of A A . In 2017 A. Tripathi presented another algorithm for solving the same problem. This article presents an algorithm whose foundation is the same as Tripathi’s. However, this algorithm is significantly different from Tripathi’s and we show that its worst case time complexity is also a logarithmic function of A A .



1
Introduction

Imagine a monetary system with coins of denominations three and five. What is the greatest integer amount which cannot be supplied exactly? The answer is seven. We can easily realize this by noting that seven can not be supplied but eight, nine and ten can since:

\displaystyle 5+3=

\displaystyle 8

\displaystyle 3*3=

\displaystyle 9

\displaystyle 5*2=

\displaystyle 10

Any amount greater than ten can be supplied by adding a number of three-unit coins to one of the amounts above. This is one example of the so-called Frobenius problem.
Given a set of integers
A=\{a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},...\;a\textsubscript{c}\}
the Frobenius problem is to find the greatest integer
g(A)
which cannot be represented as a non-negative linear combination of
A
. In the money supply problem the different denominations correspond to the elements in
A
and
g(A)
is the greatest amount which can not be supplied. J. Sylvester, who was the first mathematician to study the Frobenius problem, discovered a closed form formula in 1882 solving the problem when the set
A
has two elements. (This problem was later named after F. Frobenius probably because he popularized the problem through his lectures.)

Theorem 1
.

Sylvester’s theorem: Given
A=\{a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2}\}

g(A)=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{2}

In the general formulation of the Frobenius problem the number of variables is arbitrary. However, in this article we are presenting an algorithm solving the Frobenius problem in three variables. Below follows a formal definition of this problem. Note that
a\textsubscript{1}
is defined to be greater than one. However, this is done without any major loss of generality since
g(A)
is always minus one if
a\textsubscript{1}
equals one.

Definition

8
:

Given the set of integers
A=\{a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3}\}
where
1<a\textsubscript{1}<a\textsubscript{2}<a\textsubscript{3}
the Frobenius problem in three variables is to find the greatest integer
g(A)
which is not in the codomain of
\Gamma
defined by:

\Gamma(X)=a\textsubscript{1}x\textsubscript{1}+a\textsubscript{2}x\textsubscript{2}+a\textsubscript{3}x\textsubscript{3}\text{ where }x\textsubscript{i}\text{ are non-negative integers for all }i

The value of
\Gamma
is a multiple of
gcd(a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})
which implies that there is no greatest integer outside the codomain of
\Gamma
in the case
gcd(a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})>1
, i.e. the Frobenius number does not exist in this case. It is also well-known that the Frobenius number exists if
gcd(a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})=1
, i.e. the Frobenius number exists iff this is the case. A classic specialization of the Frobenius problem in three variables is the so-called Chicken McNuggets problem. Given that a McDonald’s restaurant sells Chicken McNuggets in boxes of 6, 9 and 20 pieces, the problem is to find the largest amount of nuggets which cannot be served. The answer is 43.

A well-known theorem, discovered by Johnson
[
5
]
, allows any Frobenius number in three variables to be computed from a Frobenius number of a set with three pairwise coprimes. This implies that, for the three variable case, one can assume that all integers of the set
A
are pairwise coprimes without loss of generality. This is also assumed throughout this article. This assumption also guarantees that
g(A)
exists. Below follows Johnson’s theorem for the three variable case. (It can also be formulated for the general case.)

Theorem 2
.

Johnson’s theorem

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{2}=gcd(a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})\tilde{a}\textsubscript{2},\;a\textsubscript{3}=gcd(a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})\tilde{a}\textsubscript{3}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle g(a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})=gcd(a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})g(a\textsubscript{1},\tilde{a}\textsubscript{2},\tilde{a}\textsubscript{3})+(gcd(a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{3})-1)a\textsubscript{1}

A closed form formula for the Frobenius problem is only known for the two variable case and Curtis
[
2
]
showed that the Frobenius number for more variables cannot be expressed by means of a finite set of polynomials. In addition to this, Ramírez-Alfonsín
[
7
]
showed that the problem in general is NP-hard. However, several algorithms exist solving the Frobenius problems when the number of variables are greater than two. These can be divided into two categories: three variable specific and general. General ones can be used to find
g(A)
for any number of variables. E.S. Selmer and Ö. Beyer invented a three variable specific algorithm which is based on continued fractions. It was later improved by Ø. Rødseth whose result was even further elaborated by J.L. Davison
[
3
]
. The worst case time complexity of this algorithm is O(a
1
) and it was the fastest three variable specific algorithm for about 10 years until H. Greenberg
[
4
]

invented one whose worst case time complexity is
O(\log a\textsubscript{1})
. There are several algorithms for the general case. E.g. A. Nijenhuis
[
1
]
developed an algorithm in which the Frobenius problem is transformed into a shortest path problem of a directed weighted graph and solved by Dijkstra’s algorithm. This and other general algorithms can be used to solve the three variable case but none of them are as fast as Greenberg’s, i.e. Greenberg’s is the fastest algorithm known today for this case.

Several of the algorithms finding the Frobenius number, including the one presented here, are based on a theorem by Brauer and Shockley
[
8
]
.

Theorem 3
.

Brauer and Shockley’s theorem

Let
[k]
be the residue class modulo
a\textsubscript{1}
containing
k
and
m\textsubscript{k}
be the smallest integer in the codomain of
\Gamma
belonging to
[k]
.

g(A)=\underset{0<k<a\textsubscript{1}}{max}\;m\textsubscript{k}-a\textsubscript{1}

Brauer and Shockley’s theorem follows from the fact that
m\textsubscript{k}
minus
a\textsubscript{1}
is the greatest integer in
[k]
, which is not in the codomain of
\Gamma
, since all integers in
[k]
greater than
m\textsubscript{k}
are in the codomain of
\Gamma
.
g(A)
then equals the greatest
m\textsubscript{k}
minus
a\textsubscript{1}
. However,
m\textsubscript{0}
can be disregarded since it always equals zero and therefore never will be the greatest
m\textsubscript{k}
, since the assumption that
a\textsubscript{1}
is greater than one implies that
g(A)
is always greater than one.

A. Tripathi
[
9
]
published in 2017 a three variable specific algorithm based on Brauer and Shockley’s theorem. The time complexity of his algorithm is, to the best of the author’s knowledge, unknown. However, Tripathi’s algorithm and the algorithm presented in this article are founded on the same theorem presented below. Note that different notation is used in Tripathi’s formulation of this theorem and that mod does not denote the modulo operator in this article. Instead, mod is a function with two integer variables,
a
and
b
(
b\neq 0
), whose value is
a
reduced modulo
b
. Deriving the algorithm presented here, we use a few well-known lemmas of the modulo function. Below we list these as well.

Given
a,b,c\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}
and
b\neq 0

Lemma 1
.

0\leq a<b\iff\text{mod(a,\;b) = a}

Lemma 2
.

\displaystyle mod(a+c,\;b)=

\displaystyle mod(mod(a,\;b)+mod(c,\;b),\;b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mod(a+mod(c,\;b),\;b)

Lemma 3
.

gcd(a,b)=gcd(mod(a,\;b),b)

Lemma 4
.

Given
b>1

gcd(a,b)=1\implies mod(a,\;b)>0

Lemma 5
.

mod(a,\;b)=a-\biggl\lfloor\frac{a}{\mid b\mid}\biggl\rfloor\mid b\mid

Definition

9
:

•

a
2
-1
is the multiplicative inverse of a
2
modulo a
1
where
0<a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}<a\textsubscript{1}
.

•

a\textsubscript{0}=mod(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},\;a\textsubscript{1})

•

F(n,r)=a\textsubscript{2}mod(a\textsubscript{0}n-r,\;a\textsubscript{1})+a\textsubscript{3}n

Note that the assumption that a
1
and a
2
are coprimes guarantees the existence of a
2
-1
.

Theorem

6
:

Tripathi’s theorem

g(A)=\underset{0<r<a\textsubscript{1}}{max}\bigl(\underset{0\leq n<a\textsubscript{1}}{min}F(n,r)\bigl)-a\textsubscript{1}

Tripathi’s theorem is proven by showing that the minimum of
F(n,r)
, for a fixed value of r, gives a distinct
m\textsubscript{k}
for each value of
r
in the interval
[0,a\textsubscript{1})
. The greatest of these minima minus
a\textsubscript{1}
then gives us
g(A)

according to the theorem by Brauer and Shockley. Note that the minimum of
F(n,r)
, for a fixed value of r, does not in general belong to
[r]
. However, when
r
is fixed to zero it always does. That is why we can exclude this minimum.

As already mentioned, the foundation of the algorithm presented here is
theorem

6
(Tripathi’s theorem). In
section

3
we show how to minimize
F(n,r)
, for a fixed value of
r
, using a sequence denoted
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
. This sequence is defined by two functions, denoted
f
and
h
, and an integer constant, denoted
\bar{n}
, which is defined based on these functions. Below we formally define
f
,
h
,
\bar{n}
and
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
and present the theorem for minimizing
F(n,r)
by means of these.

Definition

10
:

•

h(n)=mod(a\textsubscript{0}n,\;a\textsubscript{1})

•

f(n)=a\textsubscript{2}h(n)+a\textsubscript{3}n

Definition

11
:

\bar{n}
is the smallest integer
n\geq 0
such that
f(n+1)>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
.

Theorem

9
: (slightly modified)

(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
consists of all integers in the interval
[0,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
where
\bar{n}
is greater than zero. These integers appear exactly once and there are no other elements in the sequence. The elements appear in ascending order based on the value of
h
at the element.

Theorem

7
: (slightly modified)

\displaystyle\underset{n}{min}\;F(n,r)=\begin{cases}f(n\textsubscript{i})-a\textsubscript{2}r&\text{if }h(n\textsubscript{i-1})<r\leq h(n\textsubscript{i})\\
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}r&\text{if }h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})<r\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{where }0<i\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

The function
h
(see
definition

10
) defines a sequence belonging to a sequence type, which here is called arithmetic reduced modulo (ARM) sequence. Deriving the algorithm presented here, we use the characteristics of this sequence type extensively. Therefore, in
section

2
we summarise the propositions concerning ARM sequences important here. If you are not well familiar with ARM sequences the author strongly recommends that you read that section before continuing reading this introduction.

In
section

5
we show how the Frobenius number can be computed without computing the minima of
F
for all values of r by cherry picking specific values. However, the computations in that section are based on knowing what
\bar{n}
is. Therefore, in
section

4
we derive an algorithm for finding
\bar{n}
which is substantially faster than finding
\bar{n}
by simply computing
f(n)
from
n
equal to zero until
f(n)
becomes greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
. This analysis starts with concluding that
\bar{n}
equals zero iff:

f(1)=a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

After having concluded that we assume that the opposite is true and show that
f(n)
is, in this case, decreasing within the interval
[\check{n}\textsubscript{i},\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}]
for any
i
if
h
is decreasing, where
\check{n}\textsubscript{i}
is a lower border of
h
and
\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}
is an upper border. This implies that the smallest integer
n
, such that
f(n)
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, equals
\check{n}\textsubscript{i}
for some
i
. This in turn implies that
\bar{n}
equals
\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}
for some positive integer
i
. When
h
is increasing then
f(n)
is increasing within the interval
[\check{n}\textsubscript{i},\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}]
for any
i
. We can also show that
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
f(n)
for all
n
in the interval
[\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1},\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1})
. This gives us two cases when
h
is increasing. Either
n
, such that
f(n)
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, is not greater than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
or it equals
\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}
for some
i
greater than one. All in all, to find
\bar{n}
we want to find the smallest
i
such that
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
when
h
is increasing and the smallest
i
such that
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
when
h
is decreasing. Below follows a lemma stating how this can be done by means of the border sequence of
h\textsuperscript{s}
together with the definition of the function defining this sequence, which we denote
e
. (The constant
\theta
is derived from the set
A
.)

Definition

12
:

\displaystyle e(i)=mod(\bar{\alpha}i,\;\alpha)\text{ where }\left\{\begin{aligned} 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&\alpha=a\textsubscript{0}\\
&\bar{\alpha}=mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)\\
\\
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&\alpha=a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}\\
&\bar{\alpha}=\alpha-mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)\end{aligned}\right.

Lemma

36
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\\
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\\
\end{aligned}\iff\frac{e(i)}{i}>\theta\text{ where }0<i<\alpha

As
lemma

36
shows
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, when
h
is increasing, if the ratio
e(i)/i
is less than
\theta
and the same condition determines whether
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, when
h
is decreasing, i.e. we can find
\bar{n}
if we find the smallest integer
i
fulfilling this condition. In
section

2
an algorithm is described finding the first element in an ARM sequence such that the ratio of the element and its index is less than a constant. Using this algorithm allows us to derive an algorithm
finding
\bar{n}
which worst case time complexity is
O(\log a\textsubscript{0})
. This implies that finding
\bar{n}
using this algorithm is substantially faster than finding
\bar{n}
by simply computing
f(n)
from
n
equal to zero until
f(n)
becomes greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
. The algorithm finding the first element in an ARM sequence, such that the ratio of the element and its index is less than a constant, is based on that this element can be computed by means of closed form formulas when a specific condition apply and if this condition does not apply then this problem can be transformed into an equivalent problem of the border sequence of the ARM sequence. This transformation can be repeated until we have formulated a problem where the condition is satisfied, i.e. we can use the border sequence sequence of the ARM sequence to solve this problem. This ARM sequence is in our case defined by the function
e
so the algorithm finding
\bar{n}
is based on the diff-mod sequence of
e
and the first pair in this sequence is
(\bar{\alpha},\alpha)
. Once this diff-mod sequence has been generated,
\bar{n}
can be computed by means of closed form formulas. However, in most cases we do not have to generate the entire diff-mod sequence. The closed form formulas for computing
\bar{n}
are based on a specific pair in the diff-mod sequence, denoted
(\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma$},\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma$})
, and its predecessor.
After generating the diff-mod sequence until the pair
(\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma$},\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma$})
,
\bar{n}
can be computed by means of closed form formulas. Further down we describe the complete algorithm
presented here and list all formulas for computing
\bar{n}
.

Equipped with an efficient algorithm for computing
\bar{n}
, we derive in
section

5
formulas for computing the Frobenius number. In total six formulas are derived for as many mutually exclusive cases. Jointly these cover all possible cases. First we derive a formula for the case where
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
. (Further down we describe the complete algorithm presented here and list the formulas for all cases.) Then we take a closer look at
theorem

6
(Tripathi’s theorem) to see how we can cherry pick values of
r
to compute the Frobenius number. Doing that gives us the following formula for doing that.

Definition

16
:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(n\textsubscript{i})-h(n\textsubscript{i-1})\text{ where }0<i\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

Lemma

46
:

g(A)=max\Bigl(\underset{i}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1),a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})-1)\Bigl)-a\textsubscript{1}\text{ where }0<i\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

The formula given by
lemma

46
, consists of a maximum of two expressions minus
a\textsubscript{1}
. We label these expressions A and B:

\displaystyle A:

\displaystyle\quad\underset{i}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)

\displaystyle B:

\displaystyle\quad a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})-1)

Then we analyse the case where
h
is increasing and
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
and show that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
is constant for all
i
. This implies that A is given by
i
such that
n\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{n}
. This implies that the Frobenius number is given by either expression A and B. Then we show that A will always be greater than B for the remaining cases, i.e. B can be neglected for these cases.

A is a maximum of a number of candidates. It is trivial to realize that the greater
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
are the stronger the corresponding candidate is to be the maximum of A. Therefore, we analyse how the size of
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
are correlated and prove a lemma which will help us greatly to understand this correlation.

Definition

17
:

•

\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\text{ is the smallest integer }\Delta>0\text{ such that }h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)\leq n\textsubscript{i}.

•

\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\text{ is the smallest integer }\Delta>0\text{ such that }h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}.

Lemma

48
: (shortened)

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=min(\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i},\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})

The lemma above implies that
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
increases or stays the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases since
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
is the index of the first element in an ARM sequence lower than a limit which decreases when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases. Equivalent reasoning yields that
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
decreases or stays the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases. We also show that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals one and that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{n}
. This implies that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
increases or stays the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases as long as
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. However,
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
will equal
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
at one point and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
will then equal
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
from that point onwards and decrease or stay the same. Deeper analysis shows that the number of values of
i
, which potentially can give the maximum of A, can be reduced to two in all cases except for one case where there is only one possible option. To derive formulas for the remaining cases, for which
\bar{\alpha}
is greater than
\theta
, we use an algorithm described in
section

2
. This algorithm finds the first element in an ARM sequence not greater than a limit. It is based on that this element will be one of the local minima of the sequence if the limit is not greater than the greatest local minima. This is the case if the modulus of the border sequence of the ARM sequence is greater than the limit and then the problem can be transformed into the problem of finding the first element of the border sequence less than the limit. This transformation can be repeated as long as the limit is not greater than the greatest local minima, i.e. we can again use the border sequence sequence of the ARM sequence to solve this problem. Here we use this approach to compute
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. Therefore, we have to compute the diff-mod sequence of the sequence defined by
h\textsubscript{-1}
. The first pair of this diff-mod sequence equals
(a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1},a\textsubscript{1})
and we denote the pairs in this sequence
(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j},\varphi\textsubscript{j})
. In addition, we need the diff-mod sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}
to compute
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. However, each pair in this sequence is given by the diff-mod sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}
(see
lemma

18
) so we do not need to compute it. Often we do not have to generate the entire diff-mod sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}
either. The closed form formulas for computing
g(A)
are based on two consecutive pairs which moduli are defined by:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

Below we summarize the complete algorithm presented here and list formulas yielding
g(A)
for all cases.

Complete algorithm for computing the Frobenius number:

1.

Compute
a\textsubscript{0}=mod(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},\;a\textsubscript{1})\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{IN4}\lx@text@rbrace}

2.

If
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
then:

g(A)=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{1}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FF1}\lx@text@rbrace}

3.

else if
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
then:

(a)

compute (see
definitions

12
and
13
):

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad

\displaystyle\alpha=a\textsubscript{0}

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}=mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)

\displaystyle\beta=a\textsubscript{2}\alpha+a\textsubscript{3}

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad

\displaystyle\alpha=a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}=\alpha-mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)

\displaystyle\beta=a\textsubscript{2}\alpha-a\textsubscript{3}

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\beta}

(b)

if
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}<\theta
:

{
Theorem

8
}

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\bar{n}$}=\biggl\lceil\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}{\beta}\biggl\rceil-1

{
Theorem

12
}

\displaystyle\quad g(A)=max(\begin{aligned} &a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{0}-1),\\
&a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-\text{$\bar{n}$}a\textsubscript{0}-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}

(c)

else if
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}<\theta
:

{
Theorem

8
}

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\bar{n}$}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor

{
Theorem

13
}

\displaystyle\quad g(A)=max(\begin{aligned} &a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)-1),\\
&a\textsubscript{3}(\text{$\bar{n}$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(\alpha-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}

(d)

else if
\bar{\alpha}>\theta

i.

compute the diff-mod sequence (see
definition

4
) starting with the pair
(\bar{\alpha},\alpha)
until the pair
(\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma$},\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma$})
, where
\sigma
is the smallest integer
j
such that
\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{j}
is less than
\theta\textsubscript{j}
, where
\theta\textsubscript{j}
is defined by:

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FN2}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad 2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{j}<\alpha\textsubscript{j}:\quad

\displaystyle\theta\textsubscript{j+1}=\frac{\alpha\textsubscript{j}\theta\textsubscript{j}}{\alpha\textsubscript{j+1}-\theta\textsubscript{j}}

\displaystyle 2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{j}>\alpha\textsubscript{j}:\quad

\displaystyle\theta\textsubscript{j+1}=\frac{\alpha\textsubscript{j}\theta\textsubscript{j}}{\alpha\textsubscript{j+1}+\theta\textsubscript{j}}

\displaystyle\theta\textsubscript{1}=\theta

ii.

compute
\bar{n}
(see
theorem

10
):

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}=h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}+a\textsubscript{0})\text{ if }2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}<\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}=h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}\left(\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}-\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\biggl\lceil\frac{\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}{\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma$}+\theta\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}\biggl\rceil+a\textsubscript{0}\right)\text{ if }2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}>\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}

iii.

compute the diff-mod sequence starting with the pair
(a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1},a\textsubscript{1})
until the pair
(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$},\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})
defined by:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FF14}\lx@text@rbrace}

iv.

compute
g(A)
:

{
Theorem

14
}

\displaystyle\text{ if }\text{$\bar{n}$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1:

\displaystyle\quad g(A)=max(\begin{aligned} &a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1),\\
&a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h\textsuperscript{s}(\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}

{
Theorem

15
}

\displaystyle\text{ else if }\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1\text{ and }2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}:

\displaystyle\quad g(A)=max(\begin{aligned} &a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1),\\
&a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h\textsuperscript{s}(\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\text{where }\dot{\epsilon}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}\biggl\rfloor

{
Theorem

16
}

\displaystyle\text{ else if }\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1\text{, }2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}\text{ and }\text{$\bar{n}$}=\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1:

\displaystyle\quad g(A)=a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1)-a\textsubscript{1}

{
Theorem

16
}

\displaystyle\text{ else if }\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1\text{, }2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}\text{ and }\text{$\bar{n}$}>\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1:

\displaystyle\quad g(A)=max(\begin{aligned} &a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1),\\
&a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)+\\
&a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\\
\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\text{where }\bar{\epsilon}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}+1-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}\biggl\rfloor\text{ for the last two cases}

Note that the algorithm above covers all possible cases since:

•

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}\neq a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM24}\lx@text@rbrace}

•

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\text{ if }2a\textsubscript{0}=a\textsubscript{1}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM25}\lx@text@rbrace}

•

\bar{\alpha}\neq\theta\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM9}\lx@text@rbrace}

•

2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}\neq\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FN11}\lx@text@rbrace}

•

\bar{\alpha}<\theta
if
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FF24}\lx@text@rbrace}

You might also have noted that the formula for the case
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
is the same as the one given by Sylvester’s theorem (
theorem

1
) for solving the two variable case. Basically, this implies that
g(a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3})
equals
g(a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2})
for this case.

We finalize this introduction by giving an overview of the entire article. In
section

2
we summarise the propositions concerning ARM sequences important here. In
section

3
we show how to minimize
F(n,r)
, for a fixed value of
r
, by means of the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
. In
section

4
we derive an algorithm for finding
\bar{n}
. In
section

5
we derive formulas for computing the Frobenius number.
In
section

6
we provide examples. In
section

7
we finally analyse the time complexity of the algorithm presented here and conclude that it is is a logarithmic function of
A
, which makes the time complexity comparable to Greenberg’s algorithm, the fastest algorithm for solving the Frobenius problem known today.

2
ARM sequences

The function
h
(see
definition

10
) defines a sequence belonging to a type of sequence, which here is called arithmetic reduced modulo (ARM) sequence. Deriving the algorithm presented here, we use the characteristics of this type of sequence extensively. In this section we summarise these characteristics. Most of them are well-known and presented here without proof. In addition to this, we present two algorithms solving subproblems of the algorithm presented in this article. The first algorithm finds the first element in an ARM sequence not greater than a specific limit. The second one finds the first element in an ARM sequence such that the ratio of the element and its index is not greater than a specific limit. Proofs of these algorithms are given. We also analyse the time complexity of these algorithms.

The sequence defined by
h
and all other ARM sequences presented here meet specific criteria. ARM sequences have two parameters, here called difference and modulus. The criteria is that these parameters are positive coprimes and that the difference is less than the modulus. Below follows a formal definition of the ARM sequences discussed here.

Definition 1
.

The xth element in an ARM sequence, denoted
u(x)
, is defined by:

u(x)=mod(px,\;q)\text{ where }p\text{ (difference) and }q\text{ (modulus) are parameters.}

Assumption 1
.

In this article we assume that:

0<p<q\text{ and }gcd(p,q)=1

Specializations of
u(x)
can be grouped into pairs, where two specializations make a pair if they have the same modulus
(q-parameter)
and the sum of their differences
(p-parameters)
equals the modulus. Note that specializations whose difference equals half the modulus do not have any other specialization to pair up with. These sequences will be discussed later on. Looking at two sequences, which form a pair, one will have a difference which is less than half the modulus and the other one will have a difference which is greater. Here we call the sequences of a pair siblings.
We also define two categories of ARM sequences, where the specializations whose difference is less than half the modulus are called increasing and the ones whose difference is greater are called decreasing. This means that a sibling pair consists of an increasing sequence and a decreasing one. ARM sequences are periodic, with cycle length equal to the modulus, and the naming comes from the fact that their cycles can be divided into subsequences which all either are increasing or decreasing. We start with describing increasing sequences. In this case, a cycle can be divided into subsequences which all are increasing and together cover an entire cycle. The first element of such a subsequence is
q
minus
p
units less than its predecessor. All other elements in the subsequence are
p
units greater than its predecessor, i.e. the first element is a local minimum. The last element of the subsequence is greater than its successor, i.e. its a local maximum. This implies that you get the difference between an element in a subsequence and an element in the next subsequences by multiplying the number of steps between the two elements by
p
and deduct
q
. As the element with index zero always is zero, this implies that any element can be computed by multiplying its index by
p
and subtract
q
multiplied by the number of subsequences after the first one until the subsequence of the element. The index of the first element in a subsequence is here denoted
\check{x}\textsubscript{i}
whereas the last is denoted
\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}
where
i
specifies the subsequence. (We will explain the number scheme of
i
later on.) These indexes are here referred to as lower and upper borders and the corresponding elements are referred to as lower and upper border elements, denoted
u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})
and
u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$})
respectively. All lower border elements are less than the difference. The assumption that the difference and modulus are coprimes, implies that the integers in the interval
[0,q)
exist exactly once in a cycle, i.e. two equal elements within a cycle must be the same element. This implies that the number of lower border elements in a cycle equals the difference, i.e. the number of subsequences equals the difference. The upper border elements, on the other hand, are not less than the modulus minus the difference. The element with index zero will always equal zero which implies that it will be a lower border element and in fact the smallest lower border element. We number the corresponding subsequence one and denote the index of the lower border
\check{x}\textsubscript{1}
to indicate that it is the lower border of the first subsequence. The last upper border element equals
q
minus
p
, i.e. it is the smallest one.

Next let us look at an example of an increasing sequence. The function
mod(5x,\;13)
defines the sequence:

0,5,10,2,7,12,4,9,1,6,11,3,8,...

The elements above complete a cycle. Note that the modulus is 13 and that the integers in the interval
[0,13)
exist exactly once in the cycle. This cycle consists of five (the difference) increasing subsequences , where the elements increase five units compared to its predecessor. The lower border elements of these subsequences consist of the integers in the interval
[0,5)
and these are eight units (modulus minus difference) less than their predecessor. The upper border elements consist of the integers in the interval
[8,13)
and the last one is the smallest one equal to the modulus minus the difference, i.e. eight.

Now we will take a look at the sibling sequence defined by the function
mod(8x,\;13)
which gives the sequence:

8,3,11,6,1,9,4,12,7,2,10,5,0,...

Comparing carefully the sibling sequences above you will see that the decreasing one is the reverse of the increasing one. This is true in general for siblings. However, the start of the cycle of the decreasing sequence must be chosen with care. For increasing sequences we decided that the first subsequence of a cycle starts at index zero. To make the decreasing sequence the reverse of the increasing sibling the first subsequence should start at index one. The last element in a cycle will then have index
q
whereas the last element of the increasing sibling has index
q
minus one. Considering the fact that the sum of the differences of sibling sequences equals the modulus and that the decreasing sequence is the reverse of the increasing sibling, it is trivial to realize that the cycle of a decreasing sequence consists of
q
minus
p
decreasing subsequences where the first element of such a subsequence is
p
units greater than its predecessor, i.e. it is a local maximum. All other elements in the subsequence are
q
minus
p
units less than its predecessor. The last element of the subsequence is less than its successor, i.e. it is a local minimum. This implies that you get the difference between an element in a subsequence and an element in the next subsequences by multiplying the number of steps between the two elements by
(q-p)
and subtract this product from
q
. As the element with index zero always is zero, any element in the sequence can be computed by multiplying
q
by the subsequence number of the element and subtract
q
minus
p
multiplied by the index of the element. The lower border elements of these subsequences consist of all integers in the interval
[p,q)
and the first one is the smallest one. The upper border elements consist of the integers in the interval
[0,q-p)
and the last one is the smallest one (equal to zero). The lower borders of sibling sequences are the same except the first one which is zero for the increasing sequence and one for the decreasing one. The upper borders of sibling sequences are also the same except the last one which is
q
minus one for the increasing sequence and
q
the decreasing one. From the fact that an ARM sequence is the reverse of its sibling we can also conclude that
u(-x)
equals
u\textsuperscript{s}(x)
. This since the cycle starting at
\check{x}\textsubscript{1}
is preceded by another cycle which reversed becomes a cycle of the sibling sequence. Another characteristic of ARM sequences, which we will use often here is the fact that the sum of an element and the element of the sibling sequence,
with the same index, is always equal to the modulus unless the index is a multiple of the modulus. If the index is a multiple of the modulus then both elements are equal to zero. For instance, the elements with index 2 in the sibling sequences above are 10 and 3 and the elements with index 4 are 7 and 6. In both cases the sum equals the modulus, i.e. 13.

Now we will have a look at the specializations where the difference equals half the modulus. The assumption that the difference and modulus are coprimes actually implies that there is only one specialization which meets this criteria, namely the one given by:

p=1\text{ and }q=2

These parameter values give the following sequence which actually can be regarded both as an increasing and decreasing sequence:

0,1,0,1,0,1...

Below follows a formal description of what we concluded so far about ARM sequences.

Definition 2
.

•

If
2p<q
then the sequence
(u(x))\textsubscript{x\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}}
is called increasing.

•

If
2p>q
then the sequence
(u(x))\textsubscript{x\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}}
is called decreasing.

If two ARM sequences have the same modulus and the sum of their differences equals the modulus then these are called siblings. One of the siblings will be increasing and the other one decreasing. The sibling of
u
is denoted
u\textsuperscript{s}
.

Lemma 6
.

Increasing sequences:

•

Within a cycle of
(u(x))\textsubscript{x\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}}
there are
p
local minima and
p
local maxima, which indexes are denoted
\check{x}\textsubscript{i}
and
\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}
respectively.

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{1}$}=0,\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{$p$}$}=q-1

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}=\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+1

•

u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$})=u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$})-q+p

•

u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})<p

•

u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$})\geq q-p

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}<x<\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\implies p\leq u(x)<q-p

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\leq x,x+z\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\implies u(x+z)=u(x)+zp\text{ where }z\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}

•

u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{1}$})=0<u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{j}$})\;\forall\;j\neq 1

•

u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{$p$}$})=q-p<u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{j}$})\;\forall\;j\neq p

Decreasing sequences:

•

Within a cycle of
(u(x))\textsubscript{x\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}}
there are
q-p
local maxima and
q-p
local minima, which indexes are denoted
\check{x}\textsubscript{i}
and
\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}
respectively.

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{1}$}=1,\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{$q-p$}$}=q

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}=\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+1

•

u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$})=u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$})+p

•

u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})\geq p

•

u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$})<q-p

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}<x<\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\implies q-p\leq u(x)<p

•

\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\leq x,x+z\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\implies u(x+z)=u(x)-z(q-p)\text{ where }z\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}

•

u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{1}$})=p<u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{j}$})\;\forall\;j\neq 1

•

u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{$q-p$}$})=0<u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{j}$})\;\forall\;j\neq q-p

Lower borders of siblings are the same except for the first ones. Upper borders of siblings are the same except the last ones.

Lemma 7
.

2p=q\iff p=1\text{ and }q=2

Lemma 8
.

Given
\mid x-\tilde{x}\mid<q:\;u(x)=u(\tilde{x})\iff x=\tilde{x}

Lemma 9
.

\displaystyle x=zq

\displaystyle\iff u(x)=0\iff u\textsuperscript{s}(x)=0\text{ where }z\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}

\displaystyle u(x)\neq 0

\displaystyle\iff u\textsuperscript{s}(x)\neq 0\iff u(x)=q-u\textsuperscript{s}(x)=q-u(-x)

Lemma 10
.

\displaystyle 2p<q:\quad

\displaystyle\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\leq x\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}<x+z\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}\implies u(x+z)=u(x)+zp-q

\displaystyle 2p>q:\quad

\displaystyle\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\leq x\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}<x+z\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}\implies u(x+z)=u(x)-z(q-p)+q

\displaystyle\text{where }z\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}

Lemma 11
.

\displaystyle 2p<q:

\displaystyle\quad u(x)=px-(i-1)q\text{ where }\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\leq x\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}

\displaystyle 2p>q:

\displaystyle\quad u(x)=iq-(q-p)x\text{ where }\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}\leq x\leq\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}

The number of elements in a subsequence is here called the length of this subsequence. The length of subsequence number
i
is denoted
l\textsubscript{i}
. The lengths of a specific sequence vary with one unit. For increasing sequences, the lower border element of the longer subsequences are less than a limit, whereas they are greater than a limit for decreasing sequences. If the sequence is increasing and the modulus is greater than one, then the first lower border element, equal to zero, is always less than the limit, i.e. the first subsequence is always long. If the sequence is decreasing, then the first lower border element, equal to
p
, is always less than the limit, i.e. the first subsequence is short. This implies that
\hat{x}\textsubscript{1}
equals the short length of the sequence for both increasing and decreasing sequences since
\check{x}\textsubscript{1}
equals zero for increasing sequences and one for decreasing ones.

Definition 3
.

l\textsubscript{i}=\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}-\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+1

Lemma 12
.

\displaystyle 2p<q:\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})<mod(q,\;p)\implies&l\textsubscript{i}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor+1\\
u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})\geq mod(q,\;p)\implies&l\textsubscript{i}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor\\
p>1\implies&l\textsubscript{1}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor+1\end{aligned}

\displaystyle 2p>q:\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})\geq q-mod(q,\;q-p)\implies&l\textsubscript{i}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{q-p}\biggl\rfloor+1\\
u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})<q-mod(q,\;q-p)\implies&l\textsubscript{i}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{q-p}\biggl\rfloor\end{aligned}

Lemma 13
.

Given
p>1

\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{1}$}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor

Another characteristic of ARM sequences, that we use in this article, is that the function
u
is quasi-additive.

Lemma 14
.

\displaystyle u(x+y)=\begin{cases}u(x)+u(y)&\text{ if }u(x)+u(y)<q\\
u(x)+u(y)-q&\text{ if }u(x)+u(y)\geq q\\
\end{cases}

Lemma 15
.

\displaystyle u(x-y)=\begin{cases}u(x)-u(y)&\text{ if }u(x)\geq u(y)\\
u(x)-u(y)+q&\text{ if }u(x)<u(y)\\
\end{cases}

Next we will discuss the characteristic of ARM sequences defining the algorithm presented here. The characteristic allowing us to design such an efficient algorithm. The local minima of an ARM sequence form another ARM sequence. For instance, the lower border elements of the increasing ARM sequence above gives the sequence:

0,2,4,1,3,...

This is the ARM sequence defined by the function
mod(2x,\;5)
. The upper border elements of the decreasing sibling form the reversed sequence defined by
mod(3x,\;5)
:

3,1,4,2,0

The sequence consisting of the local minima of an ARM sequence is here called the border sequence of this ARM sequence. The border sequence of an increasing ARM sequence consists of its lower border elements, i.e. all non-negative integers less than the difference. The border sequence of the decreasing sibling consists of its upper border elements which are the same as the lower border elements of the increasing sibling but the order is reversed. This means that the border sequence of the decreasing sibling is the sibling of the border sequence of the increasing sibling. Since the border sequence of an ARM sequence is an ARM sequence, it has a border sequence, which is an ARM sequence having a border sequence and so on. It continues like this until an atomic sequence is reached. In other words, inside an ARM sequence hides another ARM sequence and inside that one another one and so on, i.e. ARM sequences are like Russian dolls. This creates a sequence of ARM sequences where all, but the first one, are the border sequence of the previous one. This sequence of sequences is here called a border sequence sequence. We denote the function defining the jth sequence in this sequence
u\textsubscript{j}
, its difference
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}
and its modulus
v\textsubscript{j}
. This implies that
u\textsubscript{1},\bar{v}\textsubscript{1}
and
v\textsubscript{1}
equals
u,p
and
q
respectively. The jth sequence in the border sequence sequence of the sibling sequence is denoted
u\textsubscript{j}\textsuperscript{s}
. This notation is actually ambiguous since it could also mean the sibling sequence of
u\textsubscript{j}
. Luckily these are the same. (However, this is not true for the last sequence in a border sequence sequence if the second last sequence has the difference one and modulus two. In this case the sum of the differences of
u\textsubscript{j}
and
u\textsubscript{j}\textsuperscript{s}
for the last sequences is not equal to the modulus.) The parameters
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}
and
v\textsubscript{j}
of a border sequence sequence form a sequence of pairs where the jth pair is denoted
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$},v\textsubscript{j})
. This sequence of pairs is here referred to as the diff-mod sequence of an ARM sequence as it consists of the differences and moduli of the sequences in a border sequence sequence. The number of definable pairs in a diff-mod sequence is finite and the last pair, denoted
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau$}$},v\textsubscript{$\tau$})
, will always be either
(1,1)
or
(0,1)
. Both of these pairs correspond to a sequence whose cycle consists only of the integer zero. All sequences in a border sequence sequence, but the last one, meet the criteria of
assumption

1
. The second last one also meets additional criteria. These are given below together with a formal summary of what we stated above and formulas for computing the local minima of an ARM sequence.

Definition 4
.

The sequence of functions
(u\textsubscript{j})
is defined by:

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}(i)=mod(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}i,\;v\textsubscript{j})\text{ where }\left\{\begin{aligned} \bar{v}\textsubscript{1}=p,v\textsubscript{1}=q&\\
\\
\text{Given }v\textsubscript{j+1}>0:\\
\\
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}\leq v\textsubscript{j}:\quad&v\textsubscript{j+1}=\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}\\
&\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=v\textsubscript{j+1}-mod(v\textsubscript{j},\;v\textsubscript{j+1})\\
\\
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}:\quad&v\textsubscript{j+1}=v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}\\
&\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=mod(v\textsubscript{j},\;v\textsubscript{j+1})\end{aligned}\right.

The sequence of sequences defined by
(u\textsubscript{j})
is here called the border sequence sequence of
u
.

The sequence of the pairs
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$},v\textsubscript{j})
is here called the diff-mod sequence of
u
.

The sequence of functions
(u\textsubscript{j}\textsuperscript{s})
, where
\bar{v}\textsubscript{1}\textsuperscript{s}=v\textsubscript{1}-\bar{v}\textsubscript{1}
and

v\textsubscript{1}\textsuperscript{s}=v\textsubscript{1}
, defines the border sequence sequence of the sibling sequence of
u
.

Lemma 16
.

•

Either (1,1) or (0,1) is a pair in all diff-mod sequences. This element is denoted (
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau$}
,v
\tau
).

•

j<\text{$\tau$}\implies 0<\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}\text{ and }0<v\textsubscript{j+1}<v\textsubscript{j}

•

j\leq\text{$\tau$}\implies gcd(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$},v\textsubscript{j})=1

•

(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau$}$},v\textsubscript{$\tau$})=(1,1)\implies\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau-1$}$}=1

(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau$}$},v\textsubscript{$\tau$})=(0,1)\implies v\textsubscript{$\tau-1$}=\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau-1$}$}+1\text{ and }\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau-1$}$}>1

•

(
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau+1$}
,v
\tau+1
) is undefined.

Lemma 17
.

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} 2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}\leq v\textsubscript{j}:\quad&u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)\\
\\
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}:\quad&u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)\\
\end{aligned}\right\}j<\text{$\tau$}

Lemma 18
.

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j}\textsuperscript{s}=v\textsubscript{j}

\displaystyle\text{Given }2\bar{v}\textsubscript{j-1}\neq v\textsubscript{j-1}:\quad\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}\textsuperscript{s}=v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}

In this article, we also use the inverse function of
u
, denoted
u\textsubscript{-1}
. As any other inverse function it undoes the action of the original function, i.e. in this case
u
, but only for argument values within the interval
[0,q)
.

Definition 5
.

•

p\textsuperscript{-1}
denotes the multiplicative inverse of p modulo q where
0<p\textsuperscript{-1}<q
.

•

u\textsubscript{-1}
is called the inverse function of
u
.

•

u\textsubscript{-1}(x)=mod(p\textsuperscript{-1}x,\;q)

Lemma 19
.

Given that
0\leq x<q

u\textsubscript{-1}(u(x))=x

As already mentioned a subproblem of the algorithm presented here is to find the first element in an ARM sequence not greater than a specific limit (see
lemma

48
). To find this element we use the fact that this element will be one of the local minima of the ARM sequence if the limit is not greater than the greatest local minima. This is the case if the modulus of the border sequence of the ARM sequence is greater than the limit and then the problem can be transformed into the problem of finding the first element of the border sequence less than the limit. This transformation can be repeated as long as the limit is not greater than the greatest local minima, i.e. we can use the border sequence sequence of the ARM sequence to solve this problem. Below we formulate this formally. Recall that local minima of increasing ARM sequences are lower border elements whereas local minima of decreasing sequences are upper border elements.

Definition 6
.

\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
is the smallest integer
i>0
such that
u\textsubscript{j}(i)\leq L

Lemma 20
.

Given
L<v\textsubscript{j+1}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}:&\quad\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$}+1$)}\\
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}:&\quad\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$}$}\\
\end{aligned}

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$})

Proof of
lemma

20
:
.

We start by assuming that
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}
. In this case, according
lemma

6
,

\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
equals
\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$(\tilde{\text{\char 16\relax}}+1)$}
since
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
is the smallest integer
i
greater than zero such that:

u\textsubscript{j}(i)\leq L<v\textsubscript{j+1}=\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}

This implies that
\tilde{\text{\char 16\relax}}>0
since
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}>0=\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}
and that
\tilde{\text{\char 16\relax}}
is the smallest integer
k>0
such that:

u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)\leq L\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}

In other words,
\tilde{\text{\char 16\relax}}
equals
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}
and
u\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$})
.

If
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}
then
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
equals
\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\tilde{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}
since
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
is the smallest integer
i
greater than zero such that:

u\textsubscript{j}(i)\leq L<v\textsubscript{j+1}=v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}

This implies that
\tilde{\text{\char 16\relax}}
is the smallest integer
k>0
such that:

u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)\leq L

In other words,
\tilde{\text{\char 16\relax}}
equals
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}
and
u\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$})
.

As
u\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$})
independent of the relative size of
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}
and
v\textsubscript{j}
we can conclude that:

u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{j+1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$})

∎

Another subproblem of the algorithm presented here is to find the smallest positive integer
x
, denoted
\bar{x}
, such that the ratio
u(x)/x
is less than or equal to a non-negative real number, denoted
L
. Next in this section, we will present and prove an algorithm solving this problem. This algorithm is, just like the algorithm described previously, based on that the solution to this problem can be computed by means of closed form formulas when a specific condition apply and if this condition does not apply then this problem can be transformed into an equivalent problem of the border sequence of
u
and this transformation can be repeated until we have formulated a problem where the condition is satisfied, i.e. we will again use the border sequence sequence of
u
to solve this problem. The number of sequences in this border sequence sequence, up to and including the first sequence which satisfies the condition, is denoted by
\sigma
. In this case the transformation of the problem from one sequence in the border sequence sequence to the next one will involve computing a new limit. The limit for the jth sequence will be denoted
L
j
. The condition we want to obtain is:

\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}=1\text{ and/or }\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

If
\bar{v}\textsubscript{1}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{1}}
then
\bar{x}
equals one. If
\bar{v}\textsubscript{1}=1>\text{L\textsubscript{1}}
then
(u(x))\textsubscript{x\text{$\in\mathbb{Z}$}}
becomes:

1,2,...,v\textsubscript{1}-1,0,1,...

Since
u\textsubscript{1}=u
and
v\textsubscript{1}=q
this implies that the ratio
u(x)/x
is one for
0<x<q
and zero when
x
equals
q
, i.e.
\bar{x}
equals
q
. Continuing the analysis of this problem we will assume that none of these conditions apply, which implies that
\sigma>1
.

Below follows a formal description of what we discussed above, i.e. the assumptions we make, the problem we want to solve and how a new limit is computed when transforming the problem to the next sequence in the border sequence sequence of
u
. We will explain the computation of a new limit in detail later on. As we will use the border sequence sequence of
u
to solve this problem, we define it in terms of this sequence. Note that
\bar{x}
equals
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}
and
L
equals
L
1
in this definition. Finally we prove a lemma guaranteeing that
\sigma
is defined and that
L
j
is also defined and not less than zero for
j
not greater than
\sigma
.

Assumption 2
.

In this section from this point onward, unless expressly stated otherwise:

\bar{v}\textsubscript{1}>1\text{ and }\bar{v}\textsubscript{1}>\text{L\textsubscript{1}}\text{ where }\text{L\textsubscript{1}}\text{ is a positive real number}

Definition 7
.

•

\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
is the smallest integer
i>0
such that:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\text{ where }\left\{\begin{aligned} 2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}:\quad&\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}=\frac{v\textsubscript{j}\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}-\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}\\
\\
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}:\quad&\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}=\frac{v\textsubscript{j}\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}+\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

•

\sigma
is the smallest positive integer
j
such that
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}=1
and/or
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}
.

Lemma 21
.

\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\geq 0\text{ if }j\leq\sigma\text{ and }\sigma\leq\text{$\tau$}

Proof of
lemma

21
:
.

We start with proving that
L
j
is defined and not less than zero when
j
is not greater than neither
\sigma
nor
\tau
. Then we will prove that
\sigma
is not greater than
\tau
.

The first proof we do by induction and assume that:

\text{L\textsubscript{j}}>0\text{ and }j<\sigma,\text{$\tau$}

This guarantees that
v\textsubscript{j}
and
v\textsubscript{j+1}
are defined. For the case where
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
is greater than
v\textsubscript{j}
it is trivial to realize that
L
j + 1
is defined and greater than zero. For the case where
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
is less than
v\textsubscript{j}
this is the case as long as:

v\textsubscript{j+1}-\text{L\textsubscript{j}}=\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}-\text{L\textsubscript{j}}>0

That this is the case follows from the definition of
\sigma
. Noting that our assumption is fulfilled for
j
equal to one finalizes our proof by induction.

Next we will show that
\sigma
is not greater than
\tau
. Recall that the last pair in a diff-mod sequence
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau$}$},v\textsubscript{$\tau$})
is either
(1,1)
or
(0,1)
. If the last pair is
(1,1)
then
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\tau-1$}
equals one. Therefore, we can conclude that there will be an integer
j
less than or equal to
\tau
such that
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}
equals one or
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}
. However, an observant reader might also have noticed that
L
j + 1
is not defined when
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
equals
v\textsubscript{j}
.
However, we can conclude that

\sigma
is defined also when there is a
j
such that
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
equals
v\textsubscript{j}
, even though
L
j + 1
is not defined in this case. This as
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}
will equal one according to
lemma

7
which implies that
\sigma
is less or equal to
j
. All in all,
\sigma
is always defined.
∎

Many of the upcoming lemmas assume that
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}
. This implies that:

j<\sigma\leq\text{$\tau$}

According to
lemma

16
, this implies that
u\textsubscript{j}
fulfills the assumptions made for ARM sequences in this article, i.e. all properties of
u
are valid for
u\textsubscript{j}
. Next we will prove a lemma to be used in the next one.

Lemma 22
.

\displaystyle 2p<q:\quad

\displaystyle u\left(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor\right)=u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$})-mod(q,\;p)=u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$})\text{ or }u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}-1)

\displaystyle 2p>q:\quad

\displaystyle u\left(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{q-p}\biggl\rfloor\right)=u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$})+mod(q,\;q-p)=u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$})\text{ or }u(\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}-1)

Proof of
lemma

22
:
.

We prove this lemma for increasing sequences. The proof for decreasing sequences is analogous to this one.

The length of a subsequence is according to
lemma

12
:

\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor\text{ or }\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor+1

From the fact that
\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+\text{$\dot{l}\textsubscript{i+1}$}=\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}
follows then that:

\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor=\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}\text{ or }\text{$\hat{x}\textsubscript{i+1}$}-1\\

We can then finalize this proof by means of
lemma

10
:

\displaystyle u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor)=

\displaystyle u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})+\biggl\lfloor\frac{q}{p}\biggl\rfloor p-q

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{INT10}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})+\frac{q-mod(q,\;p)}{p}p-q

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u(\text{$\check{x}\textsubscript{i}$})-mod(q,\;p)

∎

Next we will show how we can use the border sequence sequence of
u
to limit the number of candidates which need to be considered to find
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
.

Lemma 23
.

Given that
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

\bullet

2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}\implies\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}
equals
\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}
where
1<k\leq v\textsubscript{j+1}+1

\bullet

2
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}

\displaystyle\text{$\bullet$\;}\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\implies u\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$})=v\textsubscript{j}-v\textsubscript{j+1}\biggl\lceil\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}+\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}\biggl\rceil

\displaystyle\text{$\bullet$\;}\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\implies\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}\text{ equals }\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}\text{ where }1<k\leq v\textsubscript{j+1}

Proof of
lemma

23
:
.

\bullet

2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}
:

First we will show that the ratio
u\textsubscript{j}(i)/i
is ascending within a subsequence of
u\textsubscript{j}
except within the first subsequence where the ratio is constant. Assuming that
\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}\leq i<\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}
:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i+1)}{i+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)+\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}}{i+1}\cdot\frac{i}{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}\cdot\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)i+\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}i}{u\textsubscript{j}(i)i+u\textsubscript{j}(i)}\cdot\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}

{
Lemma

11
}

\displaystyle=\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)i+\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}i}{u\textsubscript{j}(i)i+\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}i-(k-1)v\textsubscript{j}}\cdot\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}

From this we can conclude that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
is greater than
\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}
since:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}\geq\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(1)}{1}=\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\text{ where }\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}\leq i\leq\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}

We can also conclude that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
will equal
\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}
for some k since:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}\geq\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}\text{ where }\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}\leq i\leq\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}

All in all, we can conclude that
1<k\leq\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}+1=v\textsubscript{j+1}+1
since:

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$v\textsubscript{j+1}+1$})/\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$v\textsubscript{j+1}+1$}=0\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

\bullet

2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}:

In this case it is trivial to realize that the ratio
u\textsubscript{j}(i)/i
is descending within a subsequence of
u\textsubscript{j}
, noting that
u\textsubscript{j}
is descending within a subsequence. Based merely on this characteristic, we cannot draw many conclusions about
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
so we need to analyse this case further.

We start with the case where
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}\leq\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}
which implies that:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

When
i\leq\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}
we get:

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(i)}{i}=\frac{v\textsubscript{j}-v\textsubscript{j+1}i}{i}

This implies that the ratio
u\textsubscript{j}(i)/i
is smaller than or equal to
L
j
when:

\displaystyle i\geq\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}+\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}

Hence follows that:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=\biggl\lceil\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}+\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}\biggl\rceil

Finally, we can conclude that:

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$})=v\textsubscript{j}-v\textsubscript{j+1}\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=v\textsubscript{j}-v\textsubscript{j+1}\biggl\lceil\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}+\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}\biggl\rceil

Next we will deal with the case
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}>\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}
.

According to
lemma

22
holds that:

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}\left(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\biggl\rfloor\right)=u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1})\text{ or }u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1}-1)

We will show that:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{j}\left(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\biggl\rfloor\right)}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\biggl\rfloor}\geq\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}

Hence, we can conclude that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
will equal
\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}
for some k where
1<k\leq v\textsubscript{j+1}
since:

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$v\textsubscript{j+1}$})/\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$v\textsubscript{j+1}$}=0\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}\left(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\biggl\rfloor\right)=

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})+mod(v\textsubscript{j},\;v\textsubscript{j+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})+\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}

\displaystyle\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\biggl\rfloor=

\displaystyle\frac{v\textsubscript{j+1}\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+v\textsubscript{j}-mod(v\textsubscript{j},\;v\textsubscript{j+1})}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{INT10}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v\textsubscript{j+1}\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{j}\left(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\biggl\rfloor\right)}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}}\biggl\rfloor}-\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}(v\textsubscript{j+1}\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}))-v\textsubscript{j}u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{(v\textsubscript{j+1}\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$})\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}v\textsubscript{j}k-v\textsubscript{j}u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{(v\textsubscript{j+1}\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$})\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{v\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-u\textsubscript{j+1}(k))}{(v\textsubscript{j+1}\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}+v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$})\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 0\text{ iff }\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)\geq 0

From
lemma

17
follows that:

\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}=\frac{u\textsubscript{j+1}(1)}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}=\frac{\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\geq 0

This implies that
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}
is greater than zero. As
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
is greater than
v\textsubscript{j}
,
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}
will be zero if
j
equals
\text{$\tau$}-1
, according to
lemma

16
, i.e.
j+1
is less than
\tau
which in turn implies that
u\textsubscript{j+1}
fulfills the assumptions made for ARM sequences in this article.

To finalize this proof we will deal with three different cases separately.

If
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=v\textsubscript{j+1}
then
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=1
and
v\textsubscript{j+1}=2
according to
lemma

7
which implies that:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)=

\displaystyle k-mod(k,\;2)\geq k-1\geq 0

If
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}<v\textsubscript{j+1}
and
\check{k}\textsubscript{l}\leq k\leq\hat{k}\textsubscript{l}
:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)=

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-v\textsubscript{j+1}(l-1))\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+1}(l-1))\geq 0

If
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}>v\textsubscript{j+1}
and
\check{k}\textsubscript{l}\leq k\leq\hat{k}\textsubscript{l}
:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)=

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}k-(v\textsubscript{j+1}l-(v\textsubscript{j+1}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$})k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+1}(k-l))\geq 0

∎

When
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}
and
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}
follows from
lemma

23
that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
equals
\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1}
where k is the smallest positive integer k such that:

\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1})}{\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

When
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}
and

u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1})/\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}
follows that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
equals
\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}
where
k
is the smallest positive integer such that:

\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

Next we will express this in terms of
u\textsubscript{j+1}
and
L
j + 1
.

Lemma 24
.

Given that
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

•

2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}

\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1})}{\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\iff\frac{u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)}{k}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}\text{ where }k>0

•

2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}

\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\iff\frac{u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)}{k}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}\text{ where }k>0

Proof of
lemma

24
:
.

When
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}
then follows from
lemma

17
that:

u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)=u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1})=\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1}v\textsubscript{j+1}-v\textsubscript{j}k\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}

This gives us that:

\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1})}{\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k+1}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}\iff\frac{u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)}{k}\leq\frac{\text{L\textsubscript{j}}v\textsubscript{j}}{v\textsubscript{j+1}-\text{L\textsubscript{j}}}=\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}

The proof for decreasing ARM sequences is made in an analogous way.
∎

Now when we can express the condition
u\textsubscript{j}(i)/i\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}
in terms of
u\textsubscript{j+1}
and
L
j + 1
we will use this to relate
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
and
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}
when specific conditions apply.

Lemma 25
.

•

2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}\text{ and }\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$}+1$)}

•
