2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}\text{ and }\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}

\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$}$}

Proof of
lemma

25
:
.

We prove this lemma for the case where
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}
. The proof for the case where
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}<v\textsubscript{j}
is made in an equivalent manner.

From
lemma

24
follows when
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}
that :

\frac{u\textsubscript{j+1}(1)}{1}=\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}\implies\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

From
lemma

23
follows that
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}
where k is the smallest positive integer such that:

\frac{u\textsubscript{j}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j}}

This also implies that k is the smallest positive integer such that:

\frac{u\textsubscript{j+1}(k)}{k}\leq\text{L\textsubscript{j + 1}}

Hence
k=\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$}
and
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}=\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}$}$}
.
∎

By means of the relationship between
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
and
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j+1}
, provided by
lemma

25
, we will now show how
u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$}
) can be computed by means of the border sequence sequence of
u
.

Lemma 26
.

\displaystyle\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\leq L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}:

\displaystyle\quad u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}(\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$})

\displaystyle\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}=1>L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}:

\displaystyle\quad u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\sigma$})

Proof of
lemma

26
:
.

We start with the case when
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\leq L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}
. In this case it is trivial to realize that this lemma is correct when
\sigma
equals two. We now continue this proof by means of induction and assume that:

u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{j - 1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j-1}$})\text{ where }1<j<\sigma\leq\text{$\tau$}

Note that this assumption implies that
\sigma>2
and that it is clearly correct for
j=2
. When
2\bar{v}\textsubscript{j-1}<v\textsubscript{j-1}
follows from
lemma

25
that
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j-1}$}=\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}+1$)}
since
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j-1}>\text{L\textsubscript{j - 1}}
. This implies that:

u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{j - 1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j-1}$})=u\textsubscript{j - 1}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}+1$)})=u\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$})\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}

When
2\bar{v}\textsubscript{j-1}>v\textsubscript{j-1}
then
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j-1}$}=\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}$}
since
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>\text{L\textsubscript{j}}
. This implies that:

u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{j - 1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{j-1}$})=u\textsubscript{j - 1}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$}$})=u\textsubscript{j}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}$})

We can exclude the case
2\bar{v}\textsubscript{j-1}=v\textsubscript{j-1}
since this would imply according to
lemma

7
that
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j-1}
equals one which in turn would imply that
j
minus one equals
\sigma
.
Finally, with
j=\sigma-1
we get:

u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}(\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$})

The case where
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}=1>L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}
can be proven in almost exactly the same way.
∎

Now we are ready to show how
u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})
can be computed from the diff-mod sequence of
u
.

Theorem 4
.

Given
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}=1>L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}

u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=0

Theorem 5
.

Given
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\leq L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}

•

2\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}<v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}:\quad u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}

•

2\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}>v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}:\quad u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}-v\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\biggl\lceil\frac{v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}{v\textsubscript{$\sigma$}+L\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}\biggl\rceil

•

2\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}\neq v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}

Proof of
theorem

4
:
.

The sequence defined by
u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(i)
becomes in this case:

1,2,...,v\textsubscript{$\sigma$}-1,0,1,...

This implies that the ratio
u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(i)/i
is one for
0<i<v\textsubscript{$\sigma$}
and zero when
i
equals
v\textsubscript{$\sigma$}
. This gives us:

\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FRB8}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\sigma$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(v\textsubscript{$\sigma$})=0

∎

Proof of
theorem

5
:
.

When
2\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}<v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}
follows from

lemma

23
that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}=\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{k}
where
k>1
since
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}>L\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}
and from
lemma

24
follows that
k=2
since:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(1)}{1}=\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\leq L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\implies\frac{u\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{2})}{\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{2}}\leq L\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}

Thereby, we can conclude that:

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FRB8}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}(\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}(\check{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{2})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(1)=\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}

When
2\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}>v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}
follows that:

\displaystyle\frac{u\textsubscript{$\sigma$}(1)}{1}=\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\leq L\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\implies\frac{u\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}(\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1})}{\hat{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{1}}\leq L\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}

From
lemma

23
follows then:

\displaystyle u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})=u\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}(\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$})=v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}-v\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\biggl\lceil\frac{v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}{v\textsubscript{$\sigma$}+L\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}\biggl\rceil

We can exclude the case
2\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}=v\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}
altogther since
\bar{v}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}
will equal one in this case according to
lemma

7
which is not consistent with the definition of
\sigma
.
∎

Now when
theorem

5
gives us an efficient way to compute
u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})
from the diff-mod sequence of
u
we only need to apply
lemma

19
on the result in order to compute
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}
, which equals
\bar{x}
. However, the case where
u\textsubscript{1}(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{1}}$})
equals zero must be handled differently. Computing the index by means of
u\textsubscript{-1}
will give the result zero. However in both cases the index of this element is
q
.

Last in this section we will analyse the time complexity of the two algorithms we have presented above. Both of them computes the diff-mod sequence starting with the pair
(p,q)
and then the desired result is given by means of closed form formulas based on specific pairs in this sequence. This implies that the time complexity of these algorithms equal the time complexity for computing the diff-mod sequence. We will show that the worst case time complexity for computing this sequence is the same as the Euclidean algorithm with positive remainders.

The Euclidean algorithm finds the greatest common divisor of two integers. If we assume that these integers are
p
and
q
, then according to Donald E. Knuth
[
6
]
the worst case time complexity of the Euclidean algorithm is
O(\log(min(p,q)))
, i.e. it is a logarithmic function of
p
in our case as we assume that
p
is less than
q
. This algorithm can be expressed by means of the following sequence:

\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}=mod(\mu\textsubscript{i},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 1})\text{ where }\mu\textsubscript{1}=p,\;\mu\textsubscript{2}=q\text{ and }\mu\textsubscript{k}=0

The greatest common divisor of
p
and
q
then equals
\mu\textsubscript{k-1}
.

Each pair in the diff-mod sequence can be computed by means of a constant number of elementary operations. Therefore, the time complexity for computing the entire diff-mod sequence is decided by the number of pairs in this sequence. We will now show that the number of pairs in the diff-mod sequence, starting with the pair
(p,q)
, are less than the number of items in the sequence computed by the Euclidean algorithm when finding the greatest common divisor of
p
and
q
,
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
. This will prove that computing these sequences has the same time complexity.

We start by assuming that the pair
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$},v\textsubscript{j})
in this diff-mod sequence equals two consecutive items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
:

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j}=\mu\textsubscript{i}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}=\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}

We refer to this condition as condition A which obviously is fulfilled when
j
equals one. We will now investigate how
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$},v\textsubscript{j+1})
can be derived when condition A is fulfilled and
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
is greater than
v\textsubscript{j}
. When analysing this, we will use the well-known fact that:

mod(a,\;b)=a-b\text{ if }2b>a>b

In this case
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}>v\textsubscript{j}
implies that
2\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}>\mu\textsubscript{i}
which gives us:

\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}=mod(\mu\textsubscript{i},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 1})=\mu\textsubscript{i}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+1}=

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mod(\mu\textsubscript{i},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=

\displaystyle mod(v\textsubscript{j},\;v\textsubscript{j+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mod(\mu\textsubscript{i},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mod(\mu\textsubscript{i}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 2},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mod(\mu\textsubscript{i + 1},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}

This implies that
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$},v\textsubscript{j+1})
equals two consecutive items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
, i.e. condition A is fulfilled.

Next we will analyse how
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$},v\textsubscript{j+1})
can be derived when condition A is fulfilled and
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
is less than or equal to
v\textsubscript{j}
:

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+1}=

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}=\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+1}-mod(v\textsubscript{j},\;v\textsubscript{j+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}-mod(\mu\textsubscript{i},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}

In this situation
v\textsubscript{j+1}
equals one item in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
and
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}
equals the difference between this item and the next one. We refer to this condition as condition B.

We will now investigate how
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+2}$},v\textsubscript{j+2})
can be derived when condition B is fulfilled and we start with the case where

2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}
is strictly less than
v\textsubscript{j+1}
which gives us that:

2(\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 2})<\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}\implies 2\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}>\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}>\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}\\

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+2}=

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mod(\mu\textsubscript{i + 1},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+2}$}=

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+2}-mod(v\textsubscript{j+1},\;v\textsubscript{j+2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}-mod(\mu\textsubscript{i + 1},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}-mod(\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 3},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}-mod(\mu\textsubscript{i + 2},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 4}

This implies that condition B is fulfilled.

Next we will investigate how
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+2}$},v\textsubscript{j+2})
can be derived when condition B is fulfilled and
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}
is strictly greater than
v\textsubscript{j+1}
which gives us that:

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+2}=v\textsubscript{j+1}-\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}=\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}-(\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 2})=\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+2}$}=mod(v\textsubscript{j+1},\;v\textsubscript{j+2})=mod(\mu\textsubscript{i + 1},\;\mu\textsubscript{i + 2})=\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}

This implied that condition A is fulfilled.

Now remains to consider how
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+2}$},v\textsubscript{j+2})
can be derived when condition B is fulfilled and
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$}
equals
v\textsubscript{j+1}
. In this case,
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}
equals one and
v\textsubscript{j+1}
equals two according to
lemma

7
, i.e. we have reached the second last pair in the diff-mod sequence. From the relationship between
\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+1}$},v\textsubscript{j+1},\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}
and
\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}
given by condition B we can conclude that
\mu\textsubscript{i + 1}
equals two and
\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}
equals one which in turn gives that
\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}
equals zero, i.e. the last item in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
. Since
(\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+2}$},v\textsubscript{j+2})
is the last pair in the diff-mod sequence and equal to
(1,1)
according to
lemma

16
this implies that:

\displaystyle v\textsubscript{j+2}=\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j+2}$}=\mu\textsubscript{i + 2}-\mu\textsubscript{i + 3}

Hence, we have proven that all pairs in the diff-mod sequence can be derived from items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
as long as we have not run out of items in this sequence. Since
p
and
q
are coprimes, the two last items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
will be one followed by zero. Looking at the relationships between the pairs in the diff-mod sequence and the items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
and noting that
v\textsubscript{j}
is nonzero for all
j
less than or equal to
\tau
, it is not difficult to realize that the two last items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
will result in the pair
(0,1)
, or
(1,1)
i.e. the last pair in the diff-mod sequence. It is also trivial to show that the two last items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
are the only subsequent items in this sequence which can result in the last pair in the diff-mod sequence. Furthermore, we have shown that every time we compute a new pair in the diff-mod sequence, we involve an item in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
, which we have not involved before. This implies that the number of pairs in the diff-mod sequence is less than or equal to the number of items in
(\mu\textsubscript{i})\textsubscript{i\text{$\in\mathbb{N}$}}
. Hence, we have proven that the algorithms presented above have the same worst case time complexity as the Euclidean algorithm.

3
The smallest integer in the codomain of
\Gamma
per residue class

In this section, we lay the foundation for the algorithm, solving the Frobenius problem in three variables, presented in this article. Below we define this problem formally. In addition, we state an assumption about the set
A
determining the Frobenius number, denoted
g(A)
, i.e. the number we are searching for. Due to a well-known theorem, discovered by Johnson
[
5
]
, we can make this assumption without loss of generality.

Definition 8
.

Given the set of integers
A=\{a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{2},a\textsubscript{3}\}
where
1<a\textsubscript{1}<a\textsubscript{2}<a\textsubscript{3}
the Frobenius problem in three variables is to find the greatest integer
g(A)
which is not in the codomain of
\Gamma
defined by:

\Gamma(X)=a\textsubscript{1}x\textsubscript{1}+a\textsubscript{2}x\textsubscript{2}+a\textsubscript{3}x\textsubscript{3}\text{ where }x\textsubscript{i}\text{ are non-negative integers for all }i

Assumption 3
.

The three integers of the set
A
are pairwise coprimes.

The algorithm presented in this article is based on the following theorem for computing the Frobenius number, which is also the foundation of the algorithm, finding the same number, invented by Tripathi
[
9
]
.

Definition 9
.

•

a
2
-1
is the multiplicative inverse of a
2
modulo a
1
where
0<a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}<a\textsubscript{1}
.

•

a\textsubscript{0}=mod(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},\;a\textsubscript{1})

•

F(n,r)=a\textsubscript{2}mod(a\textsubscript{0}n-r,\;a\textsubscript{1})+a\textsubscript{3}n

Note that the assumption that a
1
and a
2
are coprimes guarantees the existence of a
2
-1
.

Theorem 6
.

Tripathi’s theorem

g(A)=\underset{0<r<a\textsubscript{1}}{max}\bigl(\underset{0\leq n<a\textsubscript{1}}{min}F(n,r)\bigl)-a\textsubscript{1}

Tripathi’s theorem, is based on that the following expression gives us the smallest integer in the codomain of
\Gamma
belonging to a specific residue class modulo
a\textsubscript{1}
and that the residue class is distinct for each integer value of
r
in the interval
[0,a\textsubscript{1})
:

\underset{0<n<a\textsubscript{1}}{min}F(n,r)

As
theorem

6
(Tripathi’s theorem) states, the greatest of these minima minus
a\textsubscript{1}
then gives us the Frobenius number.

In this section, we will derive a sequence from which all these minima can be found. Below we define this sequence and prove a theorem stating how this is done. We also prove a few lemmas concerning this sequence which we will use in the continued analysis.

Definition 10
.

The sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
is defined by the following conditions:

•

h(n)=mod(a\textsubscript{0}n,\;a\textsubscript{1})

•

f(n)=a\textsubscript{2}h(n)+a\textsubscript{3}n

•

f(n\textsubscript{i-1})<f(n\textsubscript{i})\leq f(n)\;\forall\;n\text{ where }h(n)>h(n\textsubscript{i-1})

•

h(n
i
)>h(n
i-1
)

•

f(n
i
)<a
1
a
2

•

n
0
=0

•

n
m
is the last element in this sequence.

Lemma 27
.

\displaystyle f(n)\neq a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}\neq a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

Lemma 28
.

n\textsubscript{i}<a\textsubscript{1}

Theorem 7
.

\displaystyle\underset{n}{min}\;F(n,r)=\begin{cases}f(n\textsubscript{i})-a\textsubscript{2}r&\text{if }h(n\textsubscript{i-1})<r\leq h(n\textsubscript{i})\\
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}r&\text{if }h(n\textsubscript{$m$})<r<a\textsubscript{1}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{where }0<i\leq m

Proof of
lemma

27
:
.

Proof by contradiction.

We assume that:

f(n)=a\textsubscript{2}h(n)+a\textsubscript{3}n=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

This would imply that a
2
divides
n
, as gcd(a
2
,a
3
) = 1, which is absurd since
n<a\textsubscript{1}<a\textsubscript{2}
.

In addition:

f(1)=a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}

∎

Proof of
lemma

28
:
.

This lemma follows trivially from the fact that
f(n\textsubscript{i})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
and that:

f(n)\geq a\textsubscript{3}n>a\textsubscript{2}n

∎

Proof of
theorem

7
:
.

\displaystyle F(n,r)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{2}mod(a\textsubscript{0}n-r,\;a\textsubscript{1})+a\textsubscript{3}n

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{IN9}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{2}mod(h(n)-r,\;a\textsubscript{1})+a\textsubscript{3}n

\bullet

h(n)\geq r\implies 0\leq h(n)-r<a\textsubscript{1}

\displaystyle F(n,r)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{2}h(n)-a\textsubscript{2}r+a\textsubscript{3}n\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{IN8}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(n)-a\textsubscript{2}r

\bullet

h(n)<r\implies 0<h(n)-r+a\textsubscript{1}<a\textsubscript{1}

\displaystyle F(n,r)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{2}mod(h(n)-r+a\textsubscript{1},\;a\textsubscript{1})+a\textsubscript{3}n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(n)+a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}r

By realizing that the minimum of
F(n,r)
for the case
h(n)<r
is given by setting n to zero we get the following result:

\underset{0<n<a\textsubscript{1}}{min}F(n,r)=min\Bigl(\underset{h(n)\geq r}{min}f(n),a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\Bigr)-a\textsubscript{2}r

(Note that
lemma

27
guarantees that there is no risk of a tie when choosing the minima of
f(n)
and a
1
a
2
.)

This implies that provided that there is an integer
n\textsubscript{1}
such that
f(n\textsubscript{1})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

and
min\;f(n)
equals
f(n\textsubscript{1})
for
h(n)
greater than 0 then:

\underset{n}{min}\;F(n,r)=f(n\textsubscript{1})-a\textsubscript{2}r\text{ for }0<r\leq h(n\textsubscript{1})

Provided that there is yet another integer
n\textsubscript{2}
such that
f(n\textsubscript{2})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
and
min\;f(n)
equals
f(n\textsubscript{2})
for
h(n)
greater than
h(n\textsubscript{1})
then the minima for
h(n\textsubscript{1})<r\leq h(n\textsubscript{2})
equals:

f(n\textsubscript{2})-a\textsubscript{2}r

Continuing along these lines we derive a sequence of integers
n\textsubscript{1},n\textsubscript{2},...n\textsubscript{$m$}
, where
n\textsubscript{$m$}
is the last remaining integer
n
for which
f(n)
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
which implies that:

h(n\textsubscript{$m$})=\underset{i}{max}\;h(n\textsubscript{i})

From this sequence we can calculate all minima of
F(n,r)
for a specific r where
0<r\leq h(n\textsubscript{$m$})
. Note that there is no minima defined for
r=0
. This since the goal is to find the max of
\underset{n}{min}\;F(n,r)
for
0<r<a\textsubscript{1}
.

The remaining minima for
h(n\textsubscript{$m$})<r<a\textsubscript{1}
is given by:

a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}r

However, before moving on, we need to ask ourselves whether the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
is uniquely defined. When selecting the numbers of this sequence, is there a possibility of ties, i.e. can a pair of different integers
n
and
\tilde{n}
exist such that
f(n)
equals
f(\tilde{n})
? To prove that this cannot happen we assume without loss of generality that
n<\tilde{n}
. That
f(n)
is less than
f(\tilde{n})
if
h(n)\leq h(\tilde{n})
follows trivially. For the case
h(n)\geq h(\tilde{n})

lemma

15
gives:

f(\tilde{n})-f(n)=f(\Delta)-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\neq 0\text{ where }\Delta=\tilde{n}-n

∎

Next we will analyse which integers the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists of. To figure this out we will analyse the behaviour of
f(n)
. Recall that
h(n)
is an ARM sequence. Analysing the behaviour of
f(n)
, we will use the properties of this sequence type extensively. However, to do that we must first prove that
h
fulfills
assumption

1
.

Lemma 29
.

gcd(a\textsubscript{1},a\textsubscript{0})=1\text{ and }0<a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}

Proof of
lemma

29
:
.

Proving this lemma, we will use the well-known lemmas below where
a\textsuperscript{-1}
is the multiplicative inverse of
a
modulo
b
:

\displaystyle gcd(a,b)=1\implies

\displaystyle gcd(a\textsuperscript{-1},b)=1

\displaystyle gcd(a,b)=gcd(a,c)=1\implies

\displaystyle gcd(a,bc)=1

\displaystyle gcd(a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1},a\textsubscript{1})=1=

\displaystyle gcd(a\textsubscript{3},a\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle gcd(a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},a\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle gcd(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},a\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{IN10}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle gcd(mod(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},\;a\textsubscript{1}),a\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle gcd(a\textsubscript{0},a\textsubscript{1})

From
lemma

4
, recalling that a
1
is greater than one, follows that:

a\textsubscript{0}=mod(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},\;a\textsubscript{1})>0

∎

Recall that ARM sequences are increasing or decreasing within intervals defined by lower and upper borders. We will denote these borders
\check{n}\textsubscript{i}
and
\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}
respectively for the sequence defined by
h
. Next we will prove a lemma telling whether

is increasing or decreasing within these intervals.

Lemma 30
.

Given that
\check{n}\textsubscript{i}\leq n<\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}:

\bullet

2a
0
<a
1

\displaystyle f(n+1)>f(n)

\bullet

2a
0
>a
1

\displaystyle f(n+1)>f(n)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

Proof of
lemma

30
:
.

That
f
is increasing within a subsequence of
h
when
h
is increasing follows trivially. Whether
f
is increasing or decreasing when

is decreasing depends on the relative size of
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
and
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
since:

\displaystyle f(n+1)-f(n)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(n+1)-h(n))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(n)-(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})-h(n))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

∎

Knowing under which circumstances
f(n)
is increasing and decreasing, we are ready to show which integers
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists of for two special cases paving the way for the analysis of the remaining case. The first special case is the case where the sum

is greater than

.

Lemma 31
.

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\implies\text{$(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}$}=(0)

Lemma 32
.

2a\textsubscript{0}=a\textsubscript{1}\implies a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

Proof of

:
.

It is trivial to realize that zero is part of the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
and, from the fact that

equals
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
, that one is not if

is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
. Next we will show that none of the integers greater than one is part of this sequence. Showing that we will consider the following three cases separately:
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
,
2a\textsubscript{0}\ =a\textsubscript{1}
and
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
.

For increasing sequences
f
is increasing within a subsequence of

according to

. This implies that

for all

is zero. The proof goes as follows:

\displaystyle f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+a\textsubscript{2}h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{a\textsubscript{3}(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+ia\textsubscript{1})}{a\textsubscript{0}}+a\textsubscript{2}h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0})h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+a\textsubscript{1}(ia\textsubscript{3}-a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0})}{a\textsubscript{0}}

\displaystyle\biggl\{

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}>a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})>a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}\biggr\}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{(a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0})h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}(i-1)}{a\textsubscript{0}}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0\quad\forall\;i>0

In the computation above and in the continued analysis, a

appears as denominator. Therefore, we have to ensure that a

is not equal to zero which luckily follows from

.

For decreasing sequences
f
is increasing within a subsequence of

iff

is greater than

. Furthermore,

for

. As

, follows that the sequence

Finally we have the case 2a

= a

which implies that

. Since

is less than

according to

,
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists only of zero also in this case.
∎

Proof of
lemma

32
:
.

2a\textsubscript{0}=a\textsubscript{1}
implies that
a\textsubscript{0}\ =1
and
a\textsubscript{1}\ =2
according to

7
which in turn implies that:

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}=a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}>2a\textsubscript{2}=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

∎

In the remainder of this section we will analyse which integers
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists of when
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{0}a\textsubscript{1}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
. (Note that
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{0}a\textsubscript{1}
cannot be equal to
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
according to
lemma

27
.) We will as above often consider the two cases
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
and
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
separately. However, we will not have to consider the case
2a\textsubscript{0}=a\textsubscript{1}
as this implies that
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
according to
lemma

32
.

Assumption 4
.

In this section from this point onwards, unless expressly stated otherwise:

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

The next special case we will address is the one where
h(n)
only has one subsequence. Addressing this case we will introduce a constant, denoted
\bar{n}
, which will play a major role in the algorithm presented here.

Definition 11
.

\bar{n}
is the smallest integer
n\geq 0
such that
f(n+1)>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
.

Lemma 33
.

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{0}=1\implies\text{$(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}$}=(0,1,2,...,\text{$\bar{n}$})\text{ where }\text{$\bar{n}$}<\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}=1\implies a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

Proof of
lemma

33
:
.

From
lemma

6
follows for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
:

\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}=a\textsubscript{1}-1

Using
lemma

6
and recalling that a
3
is greater than a
2
, it is easy to show that:

f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{1})=a\textsubscript{3}\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}+a\textsubscript{2}\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}>2a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-1)\geq a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

This implies that
\bar{n}
is less than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
. It also implies that
f(n)
is greater than a
1
a
2
, if
n
is greater than
\bar{n}
since
f
is increasing within a subsequence of
h
. This also implies that
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
equals:

(0,1,2,...,\text{$\bar{n}$})

For the case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
we get that
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}=a\textsubscript{3}-a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})>0
.
∎

Note that the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists of all integers in the interval
[0,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
also for the special case where
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
is greater than a
1
a
2
since
\bar{n}
equals zero in this case (see
lemma

31
). In the remainder of this section we will show that this sequence consists of these numbers also for the remaining cases, although they in general do not appear in ascending order. We will do this by analysing the characteristics of
\bar{n}
. If
h
is increasing and
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
then
f(n)
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
for all integers
n
in the interval
[\check{n}\textsubscript{i},\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}]
since
f
is increasing in this interval according to
lemma

30
. Therefore, it is interesting to know for which integers
i

f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
when analysing
\bar{n}
. As we will show further down, we can get
h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
from the border sequence of
h\textsuperscript{s}
. For the analogue reason it is interesting to know for which integer
i

f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
when
h
is decreasing. We will further down show that
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
also can be obtained from the border sequence of
h\textsuperscript{s}
and the continued analysis in this section will be based on this sequence. We denote the function defining this sequence
e
and define it below together with a lemma stating that
e
defines the border sequence of
h\textsuperscript{s}
.

Definition 12
.

\displaystyle e(i)=mod(\bar{\alpha}i,\;\alpha)\text{ where }\left\{\begin{aligned} 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&\alpha=a\textsubscript{0}\\
&\bar{\alpha}=mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)\\
\\
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&\alpha=a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}\\
&\bar{\alpha}=\alpha-mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)\end{aligned}\right.

Lemma 34
.

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:

\displaystyle\quad h\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})=e(i)

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:

\displaystyle\quad h\textsuperscript{s}(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})=e(i)

Proof of
lemma

34
:
.

The proof of this lemma follows from
lemma

17
noting that the difference of the sibling sequence equals
a\textsubscript{1}
minus
a\textsubscript{0}
.
∎

Excluding the cases handled by
lemma

33
, we can assume that
\alpha
is greater than one which implies that
e
fulfills the criteria of
assumption

1
according to
lemma

16
. This implies in turn that all characteristics of ARM sequences presented here are valid for the sequence defined by
e
. Below, we formally state our assumption about
\alpha
. In addition, we prove a lemma stating how the border values of
h
can be obtained from
e
. Then we prove another lemma stating that when
h
is increasing then
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
if the ratio
e(i)/i
is less than a constant derived from
A
, denoted
\theta
. We also prove that the same condition decides wheather
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
when
h
is decreasing. Finally, we prove that the constant
\theta
is greater than one. A fact we will use later on.

Assumption 5
.

In this section from this point onward, unless expressly stated otherwise:

\alpha>1

Lemma 35
.

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&\begin{aligned} h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})=&\alpha-e(i)\\
h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})=&a\textsubscript{1}-e(i)\\
\end{aligned}\\
\\
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&\begin{aligned} h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})=&a\textsubscript{1}-e(i)\\
h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})=&\alpha-e(i)\\
\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\right\}0<i<\alpha

Definition 13
.

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\beta}\text{ where }\left\{\begin{aligned} 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad\beta=a\textsubscript{2}\alpha+a\textsubscript{3}\\
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad\beta=a\textsubscript{2}\alpha-a\textsubscript{3}\end{aligned}\right.

Lemma 36
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\\
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\\
\end{aligned}\iff\frac{e(i)}{i}>\theta\text{ where }0<i<\alpha

\displaystyle\frac{e(i)}{i}

\displaystyle\neq\theta\;\forall\;i

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}

\displaystyle\neq\theta

Lemma 37
.

\theta>1

Proof of
lemma

35
:
.

When
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
follows from
lemma

9
:

h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})=a\textsubscript{1}-h\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})=a\textsubscript{1}-e(i)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM29}\lx@text@rbrace}

From
lemma

6
follows:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})=h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})-a\textsubscript{1}+a\textsubscript{0}=\alpha-e(i)

The proof for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
goes along the same lines.
∎

Proof of
lemma

36
:
.

We prove this lemma for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
. The case
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
is proven in an analogous manner.

\displaystyle f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+a\textsubscript{2}h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{a\textsubscript{3}((i+1)a\textsubscript{1}-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}))}{a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}}+a\textsubscript{2}h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})-a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(a\textsubscript{2}\alpha-a\textsubscript{3})(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})-a\textsubscript{1})+ia\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM8}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{-\beta e(i)+ia\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\alpha}

This allows us to conclude that:

\displaystyle\frac{e(i)}{i}>\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\beta}=\theta\iff f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

In addition, as
f(n)\neq a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
according to
lemma

27
, we can conclude that:

\frac{e(i)}{i}\neq\theta

Setting
i
equal to one, we get:

\frac{e(1)}{1}=\bar{\alpha}\neq\theta

∎

Proof of
lemma

37
:
.

For the case
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
we get:

\theta=\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}}>\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}{a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}}>\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}=1

For the case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
we note that:

a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})-a\textsubscript{3}=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-(a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0})>0

This gives us:

\theta=\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})-a\textsubscript{3}}>\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})+a\textsubscript{3}}>\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}+a\textsubscript{3}}>1

∎

According to
lemma

36
the first local maxima of
f
, such that the value of
f
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, is given by the smallest integer
i
such that
e(i)/i
is less than
\theta
. (Note that
e(i)/i
can not be equal to
\theta
according to
lemma

36
.) If we denote this integer
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
then this local maximum is at
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}
for increasing sequences and at
\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}
for decresing ones. For decreasing sequences it is easy to realize that
\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}
is not only the first lower border such that
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
but also the first integer
n
such that
f(n)
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, since
f
is decreasing in the interval
[\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1},\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1}]
. For increasing sequences we can show that
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
f(n)
for all
n
in the interval
[\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1},\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1})
. This gives us two cases when
h
is increasing. Either
n
, such that
f(n)
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, is not greater than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
or it equals
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}
. Next we define
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
formally and prove a theorem expressing
\bar{n}
in terms of
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
. Note that this theorem holds for all values of
\alpha
. It will, besides helping us to show that
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists of all integers in the interval
[0,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
, also form the foundation of the algorithm finding
\bar{n}
, described in
section

4
. We also show that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
is less than
\alpha
, i.e. the number of subsequences of
h
. This implies that
\bar{n}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
, which it must be to be part of
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
according to
lemma

28
.

Definition 14
.

\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
is the smallest integer i>0 such that:

\frac{e(i)}{i}<\theta

Theorem 8
.

Given that 2a
0
<a
1

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}<\theta

\displaystyle\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} 1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}=\biggl\lceil\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}{\beta}\biggl\rceil-1<\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}\\
f(n)>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\text{ for }\text{$\bar{n}$}<n\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}>\theta

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\bar{n}$}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1

Given that 2a
0
>a
1

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}<\theta

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\bar{n}$}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}>\theta

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\bar{n}$}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}

Note
that this theorem is valid for all values of
\alpha
.

Lemma 38
.

\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}<\alpha

Proof of
theorem

8
:
.

We start with the case
2a\textsubscript{1}<a\textsubscript{0}
. From
lemma

33
follows when
\alpha
is equal to one that
\bar{n}
is less than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
and that:

\bar{\alpha}=mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)=0

This implies that
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
as
\theta
is greater than one according to
lemma

37
. From
lemma

36
follows when
\alpha
is greater than one that:

f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{1})>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}\iff\frac{e(1)}{1}=\bar{\alpha}<\theta\iff\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}=1

All in all,
\bar{n}
is less than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
iff
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
in which case
f(n)<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
if:

n<\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}{a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}}\text{ since }f(n)=a\textsubscript{3}n+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}n\text{ for }n\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}

This implies that:

\text{$\bar{n}$}=\biggl\lceil\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}{\beta}\biggl\rceil-1\geq 1

In addition, we can conclude that
f(n)>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
if
\text{$\bar{n}$}<n\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
.

We will now turn to the case
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
which implies that
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}>1
(note that
\bar{\alpha}\neq\theta
according to
lemma

36
). Hence,
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}
is defined. As
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
if
i
is less than
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
we can conclude that
f(n)
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
if
n
is not greater than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}
. We will now show that
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1)
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
which implies that
f(n)
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
if
n
is not greater than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1
. Hence
\bar{n}
equals
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1
since
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
.

\displaystyle f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{a\textsubscript{0}}\biggl\rfloor)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{a\textsubscript{0}}\biggl\rfloor)+a\textsubscript{2}h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{a\textsubscript{0}}\biggl\rfloor)

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{INT10}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}+\frac{a\textsubscript{1}-mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;a\textsubscript{0})}{a\textsubscript{0}})+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1})-mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;a\textsubscript{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}+a\textsubscript{2}h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1})+\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}-(a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0})mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;a\textsubscript{0})}{a\textsubscript{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1})+\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}-\beta\bar{\alpha}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\bar{\alpha}>\theta=\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\beta}\implies a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}-\beta\bar{\alpha}<0\Biggr\}

\displaystyle<f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1})<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

The fact that
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-1}+\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{a\textsubscript{0}}\biggl\rfloor
equals
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}
or
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1
implies that it is equal to the latter since
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}})>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
and we can conclude
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1)<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
.

Now we turn to the case
2a\textsubscript{1}>a\textsubscript{0}
. The fact that
f(1)=f(\check{n}\textsubscript{1})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, that
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
for
i
less than
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
according to
lemma

36
and that
f(n)
is decreasing within a subsequence of
h
, implies that
f(n)
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
if
n
is less than
\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+1}
whereas
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+1})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
which proves that
\bar{n}
equals
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}
. If
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
then
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
equals one and
lemma

13
gives that:

\text{$\bar{n}$}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor

∎

Proof of
lemma

38
:
.

\frac{e(\alpha-1)}{\alpha-1}\leq\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha-1}=1<\theta\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM11}\lx@text@rbrace}

∎

Next we will show that there is no integer greater than
\bar{n}
in
the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
. An integer
\tilde{n}
is not part of this sequence iff
f(\tilde{n})>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
and/or there exists another integer
\ddot{n}
such that
f(\ddot{n})<f(\tilde{n})
and
h(\ddot{n})>h(\tilde{n})
, which implies that
\ddot{n}<\tilde{n}
. The next lemma states when two such elements exist, having the same position in two different subsequences of
h
. We start by showing that if the first subsequence has the greatest lower border element then all elements in this subsequence are greater than the element with the same position in the second subsequence. Hence, it is relevant to compare the value of
f
at these elements. In addition, we show that when
h
is increasing then an element in the first subsequence is greater than an element with a lower position in the second sequence but less than an element with higher position. When
h
is decreasing the opposite is true. We will use this fact later on. Note that when
h
is increasing then the first subsequence is not longer than the second subsequence (see
lemma

12
) since the lower border element of the first subsequence is greater than the lower border element of the second one. When
h
is decreasing then the first subsequence actually can be longer.

Lemma 39
.

Given
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j})>h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
,
\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{j}
and
\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta}\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)&>h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})\text{ if }\Delta\geq\tilde{\Delta}\\
&<h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})\text{ if }\Delta<\tilde{\Delta}\\
\end{aligned}

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)&>h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})\text{ if }\Delta\leq\tilde{\Delta}\\
&<h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})\text{ if }\Delta>\tilde{\Delta}\\
\end{aligned}

Lemma 40
.

\text{Given }\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}<\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}\text{ and }h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1})>h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})

\displaystyle\left.\begin{gathered}f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}+\Delta)<f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta)\\
\iff\\
\frac{e(i-j)}{i-j}<\theta\\
\end{gathered}\right\}\begin{gathered}0<i,j<\alpha\\
\Delta\geq 0\end{gathered}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{where}\quad&\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}+\Delta\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{j+1}\text{ if }2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}\\
&\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1}\text{ if }2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}&\\
\end{aligned}

Proof of
lemma

39
:
.

When
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
then
lemma

6
gives us that:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})=h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j})-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})+(\Delta-\tilde{\Delta})a\textsubscript{0}

If
\Delta\geq\tilde{\Delta}
then:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})>(\Delta-\tilde{\Delta})a\textsubscript{0}\geq 0

If
\Delta<\tilde{\Delta}
then
definition

2
gives:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})<a\textsubscript{0}-a\textsubscript{0}=0

When
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
then we get:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})=h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j})-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})+(\tilde{\Delta}-\Delta)(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})

If
\Delta\leq\tilde{\Delta}
then:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})>(\tilde{\Delta}-\Delta)(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})\geq 0

If
\tilde{\Delta}<\Delta
then:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})<a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}-(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})=0

∎

Proof of
lemma

40
:
.

We prove this lemma for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
. The case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
is proven in an analogous manner. Note that according to
lemma

12
, the length of subsequence i is longer than or equal to subsequence j since
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1})
is greater than
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})
which implies that
\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta
is not greater than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1}
. This gives us:

\displaystyle f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}+\Delta)-f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}-\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta))

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace},\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\frac{h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1})+ja\textsubscript{1}-(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+ia\textsubscript{1})}{\alpha}

\displaystyle+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1})+\Delta\alpha-(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+\Delta\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(a\textsubscript{2}\alpha+a\textsubscript{3})(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1})-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}))-(i-j)a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM8}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta(e(i)-e(j))-(i-j)a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\left\{h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1})>h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})\implies e(i)>e(j)\right\}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA9}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta e(i-j)-(i-j)a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\alpha}

Noting that
i-j>0

since
\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}<\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}
we can conclude that:

\displaystyle\frac{e(i-j)}{i-j}<\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\beta}=\theta

\displaystyle\iff f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1}+\Delta)<f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta)

∎

Showing that there is no integer greater than
\bar{n}
in the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
, a comparison between two elements, having the same position in two different subsequence of
h
, will never be relevant for the first subsequence, since
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{1})
is less than
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
for all
i\neq 1
(see
lemma

6
). When
h
is increasing it instead makes sense to compare
\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\Delta
with
1+\Delta
as
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\Delta)
is less than
h(1+\Delta)
. For equivalent reasons it makes sense to compare
\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+1+\Delta
and
1+\Delta
when
h
is decreasing.

Lemma 41
.

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&f(1+\Delta)<f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta)\\
&\text{ where }\Delta<\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor\\
\\
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}:\quad&f(1+\Delta)<f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+1+\Delta)\\
&\text{ where }\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+1+\Delta\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1}\\
\end{aligned}\right\}\begin{aligned} 0<i<\alpha\\
\Delta\geq 0\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{e(i)}{i}<\theta

Proof of
lemma

41
:
.

We prove this lemma for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
. The case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
is proven in an analogous manner.

\displaystyle f(1+\Delta)-f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(1-\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+a\textsubscript{2}(h(1+\Delta)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta))

\displaystyle\mathclap{\left\{0\leq\Delta<\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor\implies\left\{\begin{aligned} &1+\Delta\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA28}\lx@text@rbrace}\\
&\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1}\end{aligned}\right.\right\}}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\left(1-\frac{h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+ia\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle+a\textsubscript{2}(h(1)+\Delta\alpha-(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})+\Delta\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(a\textsubscript{2}\alpha+a\textsubscript{3})(\alpha-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}))-ia\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM8}\lx@text@rbrace}=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta e(i)-ia\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\alpha}

From this we can conclude that:

\frac{e(i)}{i}<\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\beta}=\theta\iff f(1+\Delta)<f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta)

∎

Considering carefully the implications of
lemma

40
, we realize that
\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta
is less than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1}
in case the length of subsequence
i+1
is one element longer than subsequence
j+1
. However, this is only possible when
h
is increasing, since
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j+1})>h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})
. The implications of
lemma

41
are actually the same. The next lemma fills this gap by showing when
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})>f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{j})
. This lemma is easily proven following the same line of thought as
lemma

40
. Therefore, the proof is left out here.

Lemma 42
.

\text{Given }\hat{n}\textsubscript{j}<\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}\text{ and }h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{j})>h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})

\displaystyle\left.\begin{gathered}f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{j})<f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})\\
\iff\\
\frac{e(i-j)}{i-j}<\theta\\
\end{gathered}\right\}0<i,j<\alpha

The lemmas and theorems proven so far in this section form a good foundation for showing that all integers greater than
\bar{n}
are not part of the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
. However, there is missing one lemma gluing it all together and that is the lemma below.

Lemma 43
.

Given
0<i<\alpha
and
0<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i<\alpha

\displaystyle e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)\geq\alpha\implies

\displaystyle\frac{e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)}{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i}<\theta

\displaystyle e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)<\alpha\implies

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} h(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1})<&h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})\\
h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i})<&h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})\end{aligned}\right.

Proof of
lemma

43
:
.

If
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)\geq\alpha
then

e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)=e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)-\alpha<e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})
according to
lemma

14
. This implies that:

\frac{e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)}{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i}<\frac{e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})}{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}<\theta

According to
lemma

9
,
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})>0
since
0<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}<\alpha
according to
lemma

38
. If
2a\textsubscript{0}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
and the sum of
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})
and
e(i)
is less than
\alpha
then this gives us:

\displaystyle h(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1})=

\displaystyle\alpha-e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM8}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha-(e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i))

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\alpha-e(i)=h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})

That
h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i})<h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
in this case is proven in an analogous manner. The proof for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
is equivalent to the proof for
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
.
∎

Now we are completely ready to show that an integer greater than
\bar{n}
is not part of the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
.

Lemma 44
.

n>\text{$\bar{n}$}\implies n\textsubscript{i}\neq n\ \forall\ i

Proof of
lemma

44
:
.

We prove this lemma for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
. The case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
is proven in an analogous manner. Recall that an integer
\tilde{n}
is not part of the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
iff
f(\tilde{n})>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
and/or there exists another integer
\ddot{n}
such that
f(\ddot{n})
is less than
f(n)
and
h(\ddot{n})
is greater than
h(\tilde{n})
. We start by considering the integers which can be expressed as:

\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i}\text{ where }0\leq i<\alpha\text{ and }0<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i\leq\alpha

When
i
equals zero we get
\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}
which we can exclude from the sequence as
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
according to
lemma

36
since
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})/\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}
is less than
\theta
. When
\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i
equals
\alpha
we get
\hat{n}\textsubscript{$\alpha$}
which we also can exclude since:

\displaystyle f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{$\alpha$})=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\hat{n}\textsubscript{$\alpha$}+a\textsubscript{2}h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{$\alpha$})

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(a\textsubscript{1}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

Now remains to consider:

\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i}\text{ where }0<i<\alpha\text{ and }0<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i<\alpha

If
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)\geq\alpha
then
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)/(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)<\theta
according to
lemma

43
which implies that
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i})
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
. On the other hand, if
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)
is less than
\alpha
then
h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i})
is less than
h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
. Furthermore,
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i})
is greater than
f(\hat{n}\textsubscript{i})
according to
lemma

42
. All in all, we can conclude that the following integers are not part of the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
:

\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i}\text{ where }0\leq i<\alpha\text{ and }0<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i\leq\alpha

Next we will consider all integers which can be expressed as:

\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1}+\Delta\text{ where }0\leq\Delta<\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor\text{, }0\leq i<\alpha\text{ and }0<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i<\alpha

Note that
lemma

39
gives:

h(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1}+\Delta)<h(\check{n}\textsubscript{1}+1+\Delta)=h(1+\Delta)

If
i=0
we get
\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+1}+\Delta
which can be exclude from
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
since:

f(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+1}+\Delta)>f(1+\Delta)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM16}\lx@text@rbrace}

Next we will consider the case where
0<i<\alpha
. If
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)\geq\alpha
then:

e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)/(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i)<\theta

This implies that:

f(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1}+\Delta)>f(1+\Delta)

On the other hand, if
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+e(i)<\alpha
then
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1})<h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1})
which implies that:

f(\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1}+\Delta)>f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM15}\lx@text@rbrace}

Note that
\Delta<\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor
implies that
\check{n}\textsubscript{i+1}+\Delta\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{i+1}
. All in all, we can conclude that the following integers are not part of the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
:

\check{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i+1}+\Delta\text{ where }0\leq\Delta<\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor,0\leq i<\alpha\text{ and }0<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}+i<\alpha

To sum it up, all integers we have excluded from
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
jointly comprise all integers
n
such that:

\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}\leq n\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{$\alpha$}=a\textsubscript{1}-1

If
\bar{\alpha}
is greater than
\theta
then these comprise all integers greater than
\bar{n}
according to
theorem

8
. If
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
then we can also exclude the following integers
n
from
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
since
f(n)
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
for these:

\text{$\bar{n}$}<n\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}

This implies that we can exclude all integers greater than
\bar{n}
from
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
also in this case. Recalling that
\bar{\alpha}\neq\theta
, we can conclude that no integer greater than
\bar{n}
is part of
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
when
2a\textsubscript{0}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
.
∎

Finally, to prove that the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consisits of the integers in the interval
[0,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
and only these integers, we show that all of these are part of the sequence.

Lemma 45
.

n\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}\implies\exists\;n\textsubscript{i}=n

Proof of
lemma

45
:
.

An integer
\tilde{n}
is included in the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
iff
f(\tilde{n})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
and there is no integer
\ddot{n}
such
h(\ddot{n})
is greater than
h(\tilde{n})
and
f(\ddot{n})
is less than
f(\tilde{n})
. We assume that
h(\ddot{n})
is greater than
h(\tilde{n})
and that
\tilde{n}
is not greater than
\bar{n}
. That
f(\tilde{n})
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
follows from the definition of
\bar{n}
(see
definition

11
). Next we will show that
f(\ddot{n})
is greater than
f(\tilde{n})
for all positive integers less than a
1
which proves this lemma.

If
\ddot{n}
is greater than
\tilde{n}
then
f(\ddot{n})
is greater than
f(\tilde{n})
as
h(\ddot{n})
is greater than
h(\tilde{n})
. Moving forward we assume that
\ddot{n}
is less than
\tilde{n}
and that:

\displaystyle\ddot{n}=\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta}\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{i}\text{ where }\ddot{\Delta}\geq 0

\displaystyle\tilde{n}=\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\tilde{\Delta}\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{j}\text{ where }\tilde{\Delta}\geq 0

The fact that
\tilde{n}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}
implies that
j\leq\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}
(see
theorem

8
) and the assumption that
\ddot{n}
is less than
\tilde{n}
implies that
i
is not greater than
j
. However, if
i
equals
j
then
h
is decreasing according to
definition

2
since
h(\ddot{n})
is greater than
h(\tilde{n})
which implies that
f(\ddot{n})
is greater than
f(\tilde{n})
according to
lemma

30
. Moving forward we assume that
i
is less than
j
.

We start with the case
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
which we will divide into two subcases: one where
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j})
and one where
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater. We start with the former. From the fact that
h(\ddot{n})
is greater than
h(\tilde{n})
follows that:

\tilde{\Delta}<\ddot{\Delta}\leq\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor=\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA37}\lx@text@rbrace}

This implies that:

\tilde{\Delta}<\biggl\lfloor\frac{a\textsubscript{1}}{\alpha}\biggl\rfloor

This implies that
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\tilde{\Delta})<f(1+\tilde{\Delta})
according to
lemma

41
since:

0<i<j\leq\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}<\alpha\implies 0<j-1<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}<\alpha

Since
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{1})\leq h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
follows that
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{1}+\ddot{\Delta})\leq h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta})
which gives that:

f(\tilde{n})=f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\tilde{\Delta})<f(1+\tilde{\Delta})\leq f(\check{n}\textsubscript{1}+\ddot{\Delta})\leq f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta})=f(\ddot{n})

Next we look at the case
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})>h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j})
. This condition implies that
i
is greater than one. From this we can conclude that
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\ddot{\Delta})
is less than
f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta})
according to
lemma

40
since:

j-i\leq\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}-i<\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}

Furthermore,
\ddot{\Delta}\geq\tilde{\Delta}
since
h(\ddot{n})>h(\tilde{n})
which implies that:

f(\tilde{n})=f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\tilde{\Delta})\leq f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\ddot{\Delta})<f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta})=f(\ddot{n})

Now we continue with the case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
and start with the subcase where
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is less than
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j})
which implies that
\ddot{\Delta}
is less than
\tilde{\Delta}
as
h(\ddot{n})
is greater than
h(\tilde{n})
which in turn implies that

\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+1+\ddot{\Delta}\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{j}
. Furthermore, since
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{1})
is not greater than
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
for any
i
, we can conclude that
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{1}+\ddot{\Delta})
is not greater than
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta})
for any
i
. This allows us to conclude that:

f(\tilde{n})=f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\tilde{\Delta})\leq f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+1+\ddot{\Delta})<f(1+\ddot{\Delta})=f(\check{n}\textsubscript{1}+\ddot{\Delta})\leq f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta})=f(\ddot{n})

Left to consider is the subcase where
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{i})
is greater than
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{j})
which implies that
i
is greater than one and
\ddot{\Delta}
is not greater than
\tilde{\Delta}
which allow us to conclude that:

f(\tilde{n})=f(\check{n}\textsubscript{j}+\tilde{\Delta})<f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\tilde{\Delta})\leq f(\check{n}\textsubscript{i}+\ddot{\Delta})=f(\ddot{n})

∎

Theorem 9
.

(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists of all integers in the interval
[0,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
where
\bar{n}
is greater than zero. These integers appear exactly once and there are no other elements in the sequence. Hence,
m
equals
\bar{n}
. The elements appear in ascending order based on the value of
h
at the element.

Note
that this theorem is valid for all values of
\alpha
.

Proof of
theorem

9
:
.

From
definition

11
follows that
\text{$\bar{n}$}>0
since:

f(1)=a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

According to
lemma

33
,
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
equals
(0,1,2,...,\text{$\bar{n}$})
if
\alpha=1
and
lemmas

44
and
45
jointly gives that
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{m}
consists of the integers in the interval
[0,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
if
\alpha>1
and no other elements. From
definition

10
follows that the elements appear in ascending order based on the value of
h
at the element. Noting that the first element in the sequence has index zero, it is trivial to realize that
m
equals
\bar{n}
.
∎

4
Finding
\bar{n}

As will be shown in
section

5
, the Frobenius number can be computed without computing all elements in the sequence
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
by cherry picking specific ones. However, the computations in that section are based on knowing what
\bar{n}
is. Therefore, in this section we will design an efficient algorithm finding
\bar{n}
. However, first we will conclude that an algorithm for finding
\bar{n}
is not needed in two cases. When the sum
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
, then
\bar{n}
equals zero according to
lemma

31
. We can also disregard the case where
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
from the discussion since
theorem

8
provides closed form formulas for computing
\bar{n}
in this case. All in all, we can make the assumption below when trying to find
\bar{n}
for the remaining cases, recalling that
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
can not be equal to
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
according to
lemma

27
and that
\bar{\alpha}
can not be equal to
\theta
according to
lemma

36
.

Assumption 6
.

In this entire section we will assume that:

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}>\theta

Recall that
\bar{n}
can be found by means of
theorem

8
if
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
is known. The integer
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
defined by
definition

7
is defined as the first element in an ARM sequence such that the ratio of the element and its index is not greater than a constant.
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
is the smallest positive integer
i
such that the ratio
e(i)/i
is strictly less than the constant
\theta
. However, from
lemma

36
follows that
e(i)/i
can not equal
\theta
for any
i
so we will find
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
using the algorithm finding
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
. This algorithm assumes that
\bar{\alpha}
is greater than one and the constant
\theta
. This assumption is fullfilled since
lemma

37
and
assumption

6
gives:

\bar{\alpha}>\theta>1

(Note that this also implies that
e
meets the criteria of
assumption

1
according to
lemma

16
, i.e. all characteristics of ARM sequences presented here are valid for the sequence defined by
e
.)

Following the algorithm for computing
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}\textsubscript{j}}
we get
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})
by either
theorem

4
or
theorem

5
. However,
theorem

4
implies that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
equals
\alpha
which is not the case according to
lemma

38
, i.e. we can assume that
e
always is given by
theorem

5
. Next we finish this short section by showing how
theorem

5
and
theorem

8
combined allows us to compute
\bar{n}
from
the diff-mod sequence of
e
.

Definition 15
.

•

The pair of integers
(\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{1},\alpha\textsubscript{1})
where
\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{1}=\bar{\alpha}
and
\alpha\textsubscript{1}=\alpha
defines a diff-mod sequence (see
definition

4
).

•

\sigma
is the smallest positive integer
j
such that
\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{j}\leq\theta\textsubscript{j}
where:

\displaystyle 2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{j}<\alpha\textsubscript{j}:\quad

\displaystyle\theta\textsubscript{j+1}=\frac{\alpha\textsubscript{j}\theta\textsubscript{j}}{\alpha\textsubscript{j+1}-\theta\textsubscript{j}}

\displaystyle 2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{j}>\alpha\textsubscript{j}:\quad

\displaystyle\theta\textsubscript{j+1}=\frac{\alpha\textsubscript{j}\theta\textsubscript{j}}{\alpha\textsubscript{j+1}+\theta\textsubscript{j}}

\displaystyle\theta\textsubscript{1}=\theta

Theorem 10
.

•

2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}<\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}:\quad\text{$\bar{n}$}=h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma$}+a\textsubscript{0})

•
