2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}>\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}:\quad\text{$\bar{n}$}=h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}\left(\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}-\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma$}\biggl\lceil\frac{\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}{\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma$}+\theta\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}}\biggl\rceil+a\textsubscript{0}\right)

Note
that
2\bar{\alpha}\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}\neq\alpha\textsubscript{$\sigma-1$}

Proof of
theorem

10
:
.

Recall that
assumption

6
implies that
\alpha>1
in this entire section, which means that all lemmas and theorems in
section

3
made after
assumption

5
are valid.

When
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
then
\text{$\bar{n}$}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1
according to
theorem

8
. Noting that
\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}
is greater than zero and less than
\alpha
according to
definitions

14
and
38
respectively we can conclude that:

\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1)=h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}})-a\textsubscript{0}=a\textsubscript{1}-e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})-a\textsubscript{0}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM8}\lx@text@rbrace}

Since
0\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1<a\textsubscript{1}
we can get
\bar{n}
from the inverse function of
h
:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$}}-1=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{-1}(a\textsubscript{1}-(e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+a\textsubscript{0}))\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FEB5}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{-1}(-(e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+a\textsubscript{0}))

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA16}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})+a\textsubscript{0})

The same result is obtained for the case
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
in an equivalent manner and the case
2a\textsubscript{0}=a\textsubscript{1}
we can disregard altogether since
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
is greater than
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
according to
lemma

32
in this case. This proof is then completed by replacing
e(\text{$\bar{\text{\char 16\relax}}$})
by means of
theorem

5
.
∎

5
Formulas for the Frobenius number

Equipped with the efficient algorithm for computing
\bar{n}
, described in
section

4
, we are in this section ready to derive formulas for computing the Frobenius number
g(A)
. In total we will derive six different formulas for the same number of mutually exclusive cases which jointly cover all possible cases. Let us start with the first one.

Theorem 11
.

Given that
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

g(A)=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{1}

Proof of
theorem

11
:
.

When
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
follows from
lemma

31
that:

\text{$\bar{n}$}=0

This implies according to
theorem

7
that:

\underset{n}{min}\;F(n,r)=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}r\text{ if }r>0

Hence by means of
theorem

6
we can conclude that:

\displaystyle g(A)

\displaystyle=\underset{0<r<a\textsubscript{1}}{max}\bigl(\underset{0<n<a\textsubscript{1}}{min}F(n,r)\bigl)-a\textsubscript{1}

\displaystyle=\underset{0<r<a\textsubscript{1}}{max}(a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}r)-a\textsubscript{1}

\displaystyle=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{1}

∎

Moving forward we assume that the criterion for the case above is not fulfilled. (Note that
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}
is not equal to
a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

according to
lemma

27
and that this assumption implies that
2a\textsubscript{0}
is different from
a\textsubscript{1}
according to
lemma

32
.) Then we take a closer look at
theorem

6
(Tripathi’s theorem) to see how we can cherry pick values of
r
to compute the Frobenius number. Then we use this result to derive the formula for the next case.

Assumption

4
.

In this section from this point onward, unless expressly stated otherwise:

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

Definition 16
.

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(n\textsubscript{i})-h(n\textsubscript{i-1})\text{ where }0<i\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

Lemma 46
.

g(A)=max\Bigl(\underset{i}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1),a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})-1)\Bigl)-a\textsubscript{1}\text{ where }0<i\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

Theorem 12
.

Given
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}<\theta

\displaystyle g(A)=max(

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{0}-1),

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-\text{$\bar{n}$}a\textsubscript{0}-1))-a\textsubscript{1}

Proof of
lemma

46
:
.

Recall that
theorem

6
states that:

g(A)=\underset{0<r<a1}{max}\bigl(\underset{0<n<a\textsubscript{1}}{min}F(n,r)\bigl)-a\textsubscript{1}

Next we pick, for specific intervals, the r-value which is a candidate to give the maximum of this expressions.

\bullet

h(n\textsubscript{i-1})<r\leq h(n\textsubscript{i})\text{ where }0<i\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

\displaystyle\underset{r}{max}\bigl(\underset{n}{min}F(n,r)\bigl)

\displaystyle=\underset{r}{max}(f(n\textsubscript{i})-a\textsubscript{2}r)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FM2}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=f(n\textsubscript{i})-a\textsubscript{2}(h(n\textsubscript{i-1})+1)

\displaystyle=a\textsubscript{3}n+a\textsubscript{2}h(n\textsubscript{i})-a\textsubscript{2}(h(n\textsubscript{i-1})+1)

\displaystyle=a\textsubscript{3}n+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)

\text{$\bullet$\;}r>h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})

\displaystyle\underset{r}{max}\bigl(\underset{n}{min}F(n,r)\bigl)

\displaystyle=\underset{r}{max}(a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}r)

\displaystyle=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}(h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})+1)

\displaystyle=a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})-1)

∎

Proof of
theorem

12
:
.

In this case
\text{$\bar{n}$}<n\textsubscript{1}
according to
theorem

8
which implies that we get
n\textsubscript{i}
by adding one to
n\textsubscript{i-1}
since:

h(n)=na\textsubscript{0}\text{ when }0<n<n\textsubscript{1}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}

From this follows that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}=a\textsubscript{0}
for all
i
which in turn implies that:

\underset{i}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{0}-1)

This theorem then follows from
lemma

46
noting that:

h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})=\text{$\bar{n}$}a\textsubscript{0}

∎

According to
lemma

46
there are two parameters that we have to consider when computing
g(A)
, namely
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
. The greater
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
are for a specific
i
, the more likely it is that
g(A)
is given by the
following expression:

a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)-a\textsubscript{1}

Therefore, we will now spend some time analysing how the size of
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
are correlated and below we prove a lemma which will be instrumental for doing this. However, the proof of this lemma needs another lemma so we start by proving that one.

Lemma 47
.

0\leq h(n)+\Delta<a\textsubscript{1}\implies h(n)+\Delta=h(n+h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta))

Definition 17
.

•

\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\text{ is the smallest integer }\Delta>0\text{ such that }h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)\leq n\textsubscript{i}.

•

\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\text{ is the smallest integer }\Delta>0\text{ such that }h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}.

Lemma 48
.

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{i}=

\displaystyle min(\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i},\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &n\textsubscript{i-1}+h\textsubscript{-1}(\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})>n\textsubscript{i-1}\text{ if }\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\\
&n\textsubscript{i-1}-h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})<n\textsubscript{i-1}\text{ if }\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

Proof of
lemma

47
:
.

\displaystyle h(n+h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta))=

\displaystyle mod(a\textsubscript{0}(n+mod(a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1}\Delta,\;a\textsubscript{1}),\;a\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{IN9}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle mod(mod(a\textsubscript{0}n,\;a\textsubscript{1})+mod(a\textsubscript{0}a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1},\;a\textsubscript{1})\Delta,\;a\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mod(h(n)+\Delta,\;a\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{IN8}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle h(n)+\Delta

∎

Proof of
lemma

48
:
.

Assume that
\Delta=h(n\textsubscript{i})-h(n)
for some n such that
0\leq n<a\textsubscript{1}
and
\Delta>0
. From
lemma

47
follows that:

h(n)=h(n\textsubscript{i}+h\textsubscript{-1}(-\Delta))\text{ since }0\leq h(n)=h(n\textsubscript{i})-\Delta<a\textsubscript{1}

In addition, from
lemma

9
follows that:

h(n)=h(n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{1}-h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta))=h(n\textsubscript{i}-h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta))=h(n\textsubscript{i}+h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta))

If
h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)\leq n\textsubscript{i}
then according to
lemma

8
follows that:

0\leq n=n\textsubscript{i}-h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)<\text{$\bar{n}$}\text{ since }0\leq n\textsubscript{i}-h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)<a\textsubscript{1}

If
n\textsubscript{i}<h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)<n\textsubscript{i}-\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{1}
then follows that:

\text{$\bar{n}$}<n=n\textsubscript{i}-h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)+a\textsubscript{1}<a\textsubscript{1}\text{ since }\text{$\bar{n}$}<n\textsubscript{i}-h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)+a\textsubscript{1}<a\textsubscript{1}

If
h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)\geq n\textsubscript{i}-\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{1}
then
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}
. From this follows that:

0<n=n\textsubscript{i}+h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}\text{ since }0<n\textsubscript{i}+h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

According to
theorem

9
this implies that
n
is an element in
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
for
\Delta
such that
h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)
is not greater than
n\textsubscript{i}
and for
\Delta
such that
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)
is not greater than
\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}
and
n
then eqauls
n\textsubscript{i}-h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)
and
n\textsubscript{i}+h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)
respectively. For
\Delta
such that none of these conditions are fullfilled,
n
is not an element in
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
. From
definition

10
follows that
n
is
n\textsubscript{i-1}
for the smallest
\Delta
fulfilling one of these conditions and
\Delta
then equals
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
.
∎

The lemma above implies that
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
increases or stays the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases since
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
is the index of the first element in an ARM sequence lower than a limit which decreases when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases. Equivalent reasoning yields that
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
decreases or stays the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases. This in turn implies that if
n\textsubscript{k}
is less than
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
then
\Delta\textsubscript{k}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}
. On the other hand, if
\Delta\textsubscript{k}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}
then
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. Below we prove and formulate this formally. We also show that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{n}
. In addition, we show that
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
never are equal for a specific
i
, i.e.
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
is equal to exactly one of them.

Lemma 49
.

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{k}>n\textsubscript{i}\implies

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}\geq\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

\displaystyle\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\geq\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}<\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}\implies\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

\displaystyle\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\implies\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}

Lemma 50
.

n\textsubscript{i}=\text{$\bar{n}$}\implies\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

Lemma 51
.

\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\neq\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

Proof of
lemma

49
:
.

\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}
is the smallest positive integer
\Delta
such that
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)
is not greater than
\bar{n}
minus
n\textsubscript{k}
.
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
is also the smallest positive integer
\Delta
such that
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)
is not greater than
\bar{n}
minus
n\textsubscript{i}
and this limit is higher as
n\textsubscript{i}
is smaller than
n\textsubscript{k}
. This implies that
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}
is greater or equal to
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. Similar reasoning leads to the conclusion that
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
is greater or equal to
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}
.

If
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}<\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}
then follows that:

\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\leq\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}<\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}\leq\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

If on the other hand
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
then follows that:

\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}\leq\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\leq\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{k}

∎

Proof of
lemma

50
:
.

When
n\textsubscript{i}=\text{$\bar{n}$}
then
n\textsubscript{i}>n\textsubscript{i-1}
which implies that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
according to
lemma

48
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

51
:
.

h\textsubscript{-1}(\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})\leq n\textsubscript{i}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}-h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})=h\textsubscript{-1}(\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})+\text{$\bar{n}$}-a\textsubscript{1}<h\textsubscript{-1}(\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})\implies\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\neq\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

∎

What conclusions can we draw from the analysis made so far about the change of
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases? Let us assume that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
is within the interval
[1,k)
and that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals
k
. From
lemma

49
follows that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
will increase or stay the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases within the interval
[1,k)
. Futhermore, from this lemma follows that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
for all
n\textsubscript{i}
within the interval
[k,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
and that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
will decrease or stay the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases within this interval. Furthermore,
lemma

50
guarentees that there will be
an integer like
k
in the interval
[1,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
if
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals one, i.e. there is a break point within this interval, where
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
is less than this break point and equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when it is greater or equal. In addition,
lemma

51
ensures there will not be any value of
n\textsubscript{i}
in this interval where there is a tie between
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. On the other hand, if
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals one then
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
for all
n\textsubscript{i}
in the interval
[1,\text{$\bar{n}$}]
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
will decrease or stay the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases within this interval. We have actually already encountered such a case, namely when
2a\textsubscript{0}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
and
\alpha
is less than
\theta
. The next lemma proves that this is also the only case where
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals one.

Lemma 52
.

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}\text{ when }n\textsubscript{i}=1

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}\text{ and }\bar{\alpha}<\theta

Proof of
lemma

52
:
.

\bullet

Assume that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}=1
.

From
lemma

48
follows that
n\textsubscript{i-1}<n\textsubscript{i}=1
since
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. This implies that:

n\textsubscript{i-1}=0=n\textsubscript{0}\implies i=1

This in turn implies that there is no
k
such that:

0=h(n\textsubscript{0})<h(n\textsubscript{k})<h(n\textsubscript{1})=a\textsubscript{0}

When
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
then
\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
according to
theorem

8
which implies that
2a\textsubscript{0}
can not be greater than
a\textsubscript{1}
since according to
lemma

17
follows that:

h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{1})=mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0})<a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{0}

When
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
then
\text{$\bar{n}$}>\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
which implies that
\check{n}\textsubscript{2}
is one of the elements of
(n\textsubscript{i})_{i=0}^{\text{$\bar{n}$}}
and
h(\check{n}\textsubscript{2})<a\textsubscript{0}
according to
lemma

6
.

All in all, this implies that the only option remaining is that
2a\textsubscript{0}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
(Note that
\bar{\alpha}\neq\theta
according to
lemma

36
).

\bullet

Assume that
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}<\theta
.

n\textsubscript{i}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}\implies h(n\textsubscript{i})=n\textsubscript{i}a\textsubscript{0}\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}

This implies that
n\textsubscript{1}
equals one which in turn implies that
\Delta\textsubscript{1}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{1}
according to
lemma

48
since
n\textsubscript{0}
equals zero which is less than
n\textsubscript{1}
.
∎

Below we prove the formula for computing
g(A)
for the next case. However, first we show that, for this case and the remaining cases, we can simplify
lemma

46
since
g(A)
will never be given by:

a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})-1)-a\textsubscript{1}

Lemma 53
.

Given that
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
or
\bar{\alpha}>\theta

g(A)=\underset{i}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)\text{ where }0<i\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}

Theorem 13
.

Given
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}<\theta

\displaystyle g(A)=max(

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)-1),

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(\text{$\bar{n}$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(\alpha-1))-a\textsubscript{1}

Proof of
lemma

53
:
.

Assume that
\Delta=a\textsubscript{1}-h(n)\text{ where }0\leq n<a\textsubscript{1}
. From
lemma

19
follows that:

n=h\textsubscript{-1}(a\textsubscript{1}-\Delta)=h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA16}\lx@text@rbrace}

Assuming that
\tilde{\Delta}
is the smallest integer
\Delta
such that
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}
we get that:

n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}}=h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\tilde{\Delta})

This implies that
h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})=a\textsubscript{1}-\tilde{\Delta}
. As
2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}
or
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
, we can according to
lemma

52
conclude that there is an
i
such that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. Recall that
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
is the smallest positive integer
\Delta
such that:

h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}<\text{$\bar{n}$}

This implies that:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}\geq\tilde{\Delta}=a\textsubscript{1}-h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})

Hence, we can conclude that:

a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)>a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)\geq a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-h(n\textsubscript{\text{$\bar{n}$}})-1)

This lemma then follows from
lemma

46
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

13
:
.

From
theorem

8
follows that
\text{$\bar{n}$}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
. This implies that:

\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad h(n\textsubscript{i})=a\textsubscript{1}-\alpha n\textsubscript{i}

From this we can conclude that

n\textsubscript{1}=\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}=\text{$\bar{n}$}
which gives us that:

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{1}=h(\hat{n}\textsubscript{1})-h(0)=mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)

{
Lemma

17
}

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\alpha\text{ for }i>1

This means that:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}<\text{$\bar{n}$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}(\text{$\bar{n}$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(\alpha-1))

This theorem then follows from
lemma

53
.
∎

Recall that
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
is the smallest positive integer
\Delta
such that
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}(\Delta)
is not greater than the limit
\bar{n}
minus
n\textsubscript{i}
and that
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
is the smallest positive integer
\Delta
such that
h\textsubscript{-1}(\Delta)
is not greater than the limit
n\textsubscript{i}
. In both cases we would like to find the first element in an ARM sequence not greater than a limit. To do this we can use the fact that this element will be one of the local minima of the sequence if the limit is not greater than the greatest local minima. This is the case if the modulus of the border sequence of the ARM sequence is greater than the limit and then the problem can be transformed into finding the first element of the border sequence less than the limit. This transformation can be repeated as long as the limit is not greater than the greatest local minima, i.e. we can again use the border sequence sequence of the ARM sequence to solve this problem. This is the essence of
lemma

20
. We will use this lemma to compute
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. In our case, the corresponding sequence is defined by
h\textsubscript{-1}
or
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}
and the limit is
n\textsubscript{i}
or
\bar{n}
minus
n\textsubscript{i}
. Since none of these limits are greater than
\bar{n}
, we can transform these problems into finding the first element not greater than the limit in the first sequence of the border sequence sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}
or
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}
, where
\bar{n}
is greater than the greatest local minima in the sequence. We denote the sequence number of this sequence
\psi
. (An observant reader might ask himself what happens with
lemma

20
if
2\text{$\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}$}
equals
v\textsubscript{j}
. In this case,
\bar{v}\textsubscript{j}
equals one according to
lemma

7
, which in turn implies that
v\textsubscript{j+1}
equals one which is less than or equal to our limit
\bar{n}
according to
theorem

9
, i.e. this case is not relevant.)

Below we define the counterparts of
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
for each sequence in the border sequence sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}
and
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}
and denote these
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}
and
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}
where
j
indicate the number of the sequence in the border sequence sequence. We also show that
\psi
is less than
\tau
, implying that
\psi
is always defined. Finally, since we are actually not looking for the first element in the sequence, defined by
h\textsubscript{-1}
or
h\textsubscript{-1}\textsuperscript{s}
, but the index of this element, we show how
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
can be derived from
h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i})
and
h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i})
respectively.

Definition 18
.

•

h\textsubscript{j}(\delta)=mod(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j}\delta,\;\varphi\textsubscript{j})
where
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{1}=a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1}
and
\varphi\textsubscript{1}=a\textsubscript{1}
defines a boarder sequence sequence.

•

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

•

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}
is the smallest integer
\delta>0
such that
h\textsubscript{j}(\delta)\leq n\textsubscript{i}
.

•

\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}
is the smallest integer
\delta>0
such that
h\textsubscript{j}\textsuperscript{s}(\delta)\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}
.

•

\delta\textsubscript{i,j}=min(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j},\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}
)

•

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{$i,\psi$}

•

\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{$i,\psi$}

•

\delta\textsubscript{i}=min(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i},\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i})

Lemma 54
.

\psi<\text{$\tau$}

Lemma 55
.

\displaystyle\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}=

\displaystyle h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}))

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}))

Proof of
lemma

54
:
.

From
theorem

8
follows that
\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq 1
since
a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
. From the fact that
\varphi\textsubscript{j+1}
is less than
\varphi\textsubscript{j}
when
j
is less than
\tau
(see
lemma

16
) and that
\varphi\textsubscript{$\tau$}
equals one follows then that
\psi
is always uniquely defined and less than
\tau
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

55
:
.

Since
n\textsubscript{i}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}
follows from
definition

17
and
lemma

20
that:

h\textsubscript{-1}(\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i})=h\textsubscript{1}(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,1})=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{$i,\psi$})=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i})

From
lemma

19
follows that:

\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}))

In the same way we get:

\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}=h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}))

∎

The relationship between
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
and between
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and

\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
provided by
lemma

55
allows us to replace the analysis of the correlation between
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
with the corresponding analysis of
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\delta\textsubscript{i}
. However, to be able to do that we first need to prove some propositions. We need to show that the relative size of
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
reflects the relative size of
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
. In addition, we must show that the relative size of
\delta\textsubscript{i}
for two different values of
i
reflects the relative size of
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
for the same values of
i
. We also have to show that
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
is not equal to
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
for any
i
meaning that
\delta\textsubscript{i}
is always equal to exactly one of these. Below we prove all three propositions mentioned above. However, first we show how
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}
and
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}
can be expressed in terms of
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
and
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
respectively. We will use this proposition to prove the other ones. Having shown this, we have shown that the analysis of the correlation between
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
can be based on the corresponding analysis of
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\delta\textsubscript{i}
. In addition, we have shown that the conclusions which we have already made about the correlation between
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
also holds for
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\delta\textsubscript{i}
, i.e. that
\delta\textsubscript{i}
equals
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}
equals one, unless
2a\textsubscript{0}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
, and as
n\textsubscript{i}
increases
\delta\textsubscript{i}
will remain equal to
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
until a break point is reached and thereafter
\delta\textsubscript{i}
will equal
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
. Before this break point
\delta\textsubscript{i}
will increase or stay the same when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases and after
\delta\textsubscript{i}
will decrease or stay the same.

Lemma 56
.

Given
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{j+1}

\displaystyle 2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j}<\varphi\textsubscript{j}:\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=&\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}+1$}\\
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=&\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle 2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j}>\varphi\textsubscript{j}:\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=&\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{$\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}\\
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=&\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}+1$}\end{aligned}

Lemma 57
.

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\implies

\displaystyle\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\implies

\displaystyle\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}>\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

Lemma 58
.

\delta\textsubscript{i}>\delta\textsubscript{k}\implies\Delta\textsubscript{i}>\Delta\textsubscript{k}

Lemma 59
.

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\neq\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}

Proof of
lemma

56
:
.

This lemma follows from
definition

17
and
lemma

20
, noting that
n\textsubscript{i}
and
\bar{n}
minus
n\textsubscript{i}
are not greater than
\bar{n}
which in turn is less than
\varphi\textsubscript{j+1}
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

57
:
.

We assume that
0<j<\psi
which implies that
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{j+1}
and start with the case where
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j}
is less than
\varphi\textsubscript{j}
.

If
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
then:

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FF16}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}+1$}\leq\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}<\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}

If
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}>\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
then:

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}+1$}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{$\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}

For the case where
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j}>\varphi\textsubscript{j}
it is easy to show in an analogous manner that:

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}\implies\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}>\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}\implies\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}>\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}

Now follows a proof by induction where we assume that:

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}\implies\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

From this assumption follows that:

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}\implies\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}\implies\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

The base case where
j=1
is given by the fact that
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,1}
equals
\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
and
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,1}
equals
\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
which means that:

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,1}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,1}\implies\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

Finally the case
j=\psi-1
gives:

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{$i,\psi$}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{$i,\psi$}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\implies\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

In an analogous manner it is easy to show that:

\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\implies\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}>\bar{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}

∎

Proof of
lemma

58
:
.

We assume that
\delta\textsubscript{i,j + 1}>\delta\textsubscript{k,j + 1}
and
j<\psi
which implies that
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{j+1}
.
We start with the case where
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j}
is less than
\varphi\textsubscript{j}
.

If
\delta\textsubscript{i,j + 1}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
and
\delta\textsubscript{k,j + 1}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j + 1}
we can by looking at the proof of
lemma

57
conclude that:

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}\implies

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}<\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j + 1}<\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j+1}\implies

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j}<\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j}

From
lemma

56
then follows that:

\delta\textsubscript{i,j}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}+1$}>\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j+1}+1$}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j+1}$}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j}=\delta\textsubscript{k,j}

On the other hand, if
\delta\textsubscript{i,j + 1}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
and
\delta\textsubscript{k,j + 1}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j+1}
then:

\delta\textsubscript{i,j}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}>\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}$}\geq\check{\delta}\textsubscript{$\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j+1}+1$}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j}=\delta\textsubscript{k,j}

It is trivial to show that same condition apply for the two remaining cases, i.e. the case where
\delta\textsubscript{i,j + 1}
equals
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
and
\delta\textsubscript{k,j + 1}
equals
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j+1}
and the case where
\delta\textsubscript{i,j + 1}
equals
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i,j+1}
and
\delta\textsubscript{k,j + 1}
equals
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{k,j + 1}
. All in all, we have shown that:

\delta\textsubscript{i,j + 1}>\delta\textsubscript{k,j + 1}\implies\delta\textsubscript{i,j}>\delta\textsubscript{k,j}

In an analogous manner we can also easily show that this condition applies for the case where
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{j}
is greater than
\varphi\textsubscript{j}
. The finalization of this proof then easily follows by induction in the same manner as
lemma

57
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

59
:
.

The following shows us that
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\neq\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
:

h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i})\leq n\textsubscript{i}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}-h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i})=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i})+\text{$\bar{n}$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i})

∎

As we have already found formulas giving
g(A)
for all possible cases when
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
, we can assume that
\bar{\alpha}
is greater than
\theta
for the remaining ones. (Recall that
\alpha
is not equal to
\theta
according to
lemma

36
.) Below we analyse which conclusions we can make about the diff-mod sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}
assuming this. Having done that we have done the necessary groundwork for deriving the formula for the next case, which we do thereafter.

Lemma 60
.

\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=1\implies 2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}\text{ and }\bar{\alpha}<\theta

Lemma 61
.

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{i}=a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}\text{ when }n\textsubscript{i}=1

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}\text{ and }\bar{\alpha}<\theta

Lemma 62
.

\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1\implies\bar{\alpha}<\theta

Lemma 63
.

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}>\theta\implies\left\{\begin{aligned} \bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>1\\
\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>1\\
\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>0\\
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}\neq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

Theorem 14
.

Given that
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
and
\text{$\bar{n}$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1

\displaystyle g(A)=max(

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1),

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h\textsuperscript{s}(\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1))-a\textsubscript{1}

Proof of
lemma

60
:
.

If
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=1
then
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=1
when
n\textsubscript{i}=1
since:

h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(1)=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=1=n\textsubscript{i}

According to
lemma

57
this implies that
\Delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\Delta}\textsubscript{i}
when
n\textsubscript{i}=1
, which in turn implies that
2a\textsubscript{0}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
and
\bar{\alpha}
is less
\theta
according to
lemma

52
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

61
:
.

We assume that
n\textsubscript{k}=1
. Given that
h
is increasing and
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
then
\bar{n}
is less than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
according to
theorem

8
. If

n
is less than
\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
then follows from
lemma

11
that:

h(n)=na\textsubscript{0}

This implies that
n\textsubscript{1}=1=n\textsubscript{k}
which gives that:

\Delta\textsubscript{k}=h(1)-h(0)=a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}

Given that
h
is increasing and
\bar{\alpha}
is greater than
\theta
then
\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq\hat{n}\textsubscript{2}-1
.

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{k}=

\displaystyle h(n\textsubscript{k})-h(n\textsubscript{k -1})

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\leq

\displaystyle h(1)-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{2})

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{0}

Given that
h
is decreasing and
\bar{\alpha}
is greater than
\theta
then
\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq\hat{n}\textsubscript{2}
. This implies that:

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{k}=

\displaystyle h(n\textsubscript{k})-h(n\textsubscript{k -1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle h(\check{n}\textsubscript{1})-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{2}+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{0}-(h(\check{n}\textsubscript{2})-(a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{1}-h(\check{n}\textsubscript{2})

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}

Hence we can conclude that the only remaining case is when
h
is decreasing and
\bar{\alpha}
is less than
\theta
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

62
:
.

If
\text{$\bar{n}$}=1
then
\text{$\bar{n}$}\leq\hat{n}\textsubscript{1}
which implies that
\bar{\alpha}<\theta
according to
theorem

8
.

(For instance
a\textsubscript{1}=5,a\textsubscript{2}=8,a\textsubscript{3}=9
gives
\text{$\bar{n}$}=1
and
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=1
and
\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}=2
.)

If
\text{$\bar{n}$}>1
then
n\textsubscript{i}=1
gives:

\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}\geq 2-1=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FFA5}\lx@text@rbrace}

This implies that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=1
which gives:

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FF17}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\Delta\textsubscript{i}=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{1}-a\textsubscript{0}

This implies that
\bar{\alpha}<\theta
according to
lemma

61
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

63
:
.

From
lemma

16
follows that
\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}>\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>0
since
\psi<\text{$\tau$}
according to
lemma

54
. Since
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}
is not equal to one according to
lemma

60
, this implies that:

\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>1

The fact that
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}\neq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
according to
lemma

62
, implies that:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>1

The fact that
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}\neq 1
and that
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}\neq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
, implies that
\psi+1
is less than
\tau
according to
lemma

16
. From the same lemma follows then that:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>0

Finally, since
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=1
if
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}
, according to
lemma

7
, follows that:

2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}\neq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

∎

Proof of
theorem

14
:
.

Note that
\text{$\bar{n}$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1>\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}
and
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1>0
according to
lemma

63
.

\bullet

n\textsubscript{i}\leq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
:

\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}\geq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1-(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FFA5}\lx@text@rbrace}

This implies that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=1
which gives:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1))=h\textsuperscript{s}(\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})

This yields that:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h\textsuperscript{s}(\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1))

\bullet

n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}
:

\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=1
since
h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(1)=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}\leq n\textsubscript{i}
which gives:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(1))=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})

This implies that:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1)

This theorem is then given by
lemma

53
.
∎

The next formula involves a constant, here named
\dot{\epsilon}
, derived from the diff-mod sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}
. Below we prove two lemmas concerning this constant and then we derive the formula for this case.

Definition 19
.

\dot{\epsilon}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}\biggl\rfloor

Lemma 64
.

Given
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
,
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
and
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

0\leq\dot{\epsilon}<\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}\text{ where }\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}\text{ is the first upper border of }h\textsubscript{$\psi$}

Lemma 65
.

\dot{\epsilon}=0\implies\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1>0

Theorem 15
.

Given that
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
,
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
and
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}
:

\displaystyle g(A)=max(

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1),

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h\textsuperscript{s}(\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1))-a\textsubscript{1}

Proof of
lemma

64
:
.

From
definition

19
follows that:

\displaystyle\dot{\epsilon}\leq\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}<\dot{\epsilon}+1

\displaystyle\dot{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<(\dot{\epsilon}+1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1

This implies that:

\displaystyle\dot{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{IN7}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FFA5}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA16}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}<\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}

\displaystyle\dot{\epsilon}<\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}

Note that the preconditions of
lemma

18
are met since
lemma

63
guarantees that
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}
is not equal to
\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}
.

In addition:

\displaystyle\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}\geq\frac{1+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}>0\implies\dot{\epsilon}\geq 0

∎

Proof of
lemma

65
:
.

When
\dot{\epsilon}=0

definition

19
gives that:

\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}<1

This lemma then follows from the fact that
\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

15
:
.

Note that from
definition

18
and
lemma

63
follows that:

\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>0

\bullet

n\textsubscript{i}\leq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
:

In this case
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since

h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2})<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}]
(See
definition

2
) and:

n\textsubscript{i}\leq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}(1)=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2})\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}

This in turn implies that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\leq\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}\geq

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FFA5}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

\bullet

\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}
:

Note that from
lemma

63
follows that:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>1

\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>0

In this case
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}
since:

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2})

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(1)<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in(1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}]

\bullet

n\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
and
\dot{\epsilon}\geq 1
:

From
definition

19
follows that:

\displaystyle\dot{\epsilon}\leq\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}<\dot{\epsilon}+1

\displaystyle\dot{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<(\dot{\epsilon}+1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1

This gives us:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}+\geq

\displaystyle\dot{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1-(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)+(\dot{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})+(\dot{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\dot{\epsilon})\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}<

\displaystyle(\dot{\epsilon}+1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1-(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})+\dot{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-\dot{\epsilon})\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}

This implies that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\dot{\epsilon}
since:

\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FF27}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad 1<\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\dot{\epsilon}\leq\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}\text{ and }\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}

From
lemma

55
follows that:

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{i}=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\dot{\epsilon}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})+(\dot{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)+(\dot{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\dot{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

As
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
is increasing or static when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases up till
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
we can conclude that:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h\textsuperscript{s}(\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1))

\bullet

n\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
and
\dot{\epsilon}=0
:

From
definition

19
follows that:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1

(Note that
2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
according to
lemma

65
which is important since
\bar{n}
is greater than or equal to
\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
.)

This implies that
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}
since
h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2})
and:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}<

\displaystyle 2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1-(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

This in turn implies that

\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}
which gives:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}))=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})=a\textsubscript{1}-h(\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})=h\textsuperscript{s}(\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

Since
\delta\textsubscript{i}\leq\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
when
n\textsubscript{i}\leq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
and
\delta\textsubscript{i}\leq\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}
when
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}
we get the same result as we obtained for
\dot{\epsilon}\geq 1
, namely:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h\textsuperscript{s}(\dot{\epsilon}\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1))

\bullet

n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}
:

Following the same line of reasoning as in the proof of
theorem

14
we obtain:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$})-1)

This theorem is then given by
lemma

53
.
∎

Now we have come to the formula for the last case. It also involves a constant, here named
\bar{\epsilon}
, derived from the diff-mod sequence of
h\textsubscript{-1}
. Below we prove two lemmas concerning this constant and then we derive the formula for this case.

Definition 20
.

\bar{\epsilon}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}+1-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}\biggl\rfloor

Lemma 66
.

Given
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
,
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
and
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

0\leq\bar{\epsilon}<\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}

Lemma 67
.

\bar{\epsilon}=0\implies\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>1

Theorem 16
.

Given that
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
,
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
and
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

If
\text{$\bar{n}$}=\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
then:

\displaystyle g(A)=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1)-a\textsubscript{1}

If
\text{$\bar{n}$}>\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
then:

\displaystyle g(A)=

\displaystyle max(\begin{aligned} &a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1),\\
&a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}

Proof of
lemma

66
:
.

From
definition

20
follows that:

\bar{\epsilon}\leq\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}+1-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}<\bar{\epsilon}+1

Since
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
this gives us:

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}(1)<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA34}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}<\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}

In addition:

\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}+1-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}\geq\frac{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}>\frac{1}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}>0\implies\bar{\epsilon}\geq 0

∎

Proof of
lemma

67
:
.

When
\bar{\epsilon}=0

definition

20
gives that:

\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}+1-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}<1

This lemma then follows from the fact that
\text{$\bar{n}$}\geq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

16
:
.

\bullet

\bar{\epsilon}=0
:

From
definition

20
follows that:

\frac{\text{$\bar{n}$}+1-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}<1

From
definition

18
follows that:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1

From
lemma

65
follows that
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>1
.

If
n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
then
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}
since:

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}<

\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}(1)

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BS11}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(n)\;\forall\;n\in[1,\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}]\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA20}\lx@text@rbrace}

This in turn implies that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}\leq\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}
since:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}>

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FFA5}\lx@text@rbrace}\quad=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2})

If
n\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
then
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}
since:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}<

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1-(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in(1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}]

For
n\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
this gives that :

\displaystyle\Delta\textsubscript{i}=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\check{\delta}\textsubscript{2}))\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{FF17}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(h\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\textsuperscript{s}(1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsuperscript{s}(\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{1}-h\textsuperscript{s}(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA16}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})\quad\text{\lx@text@lbrace\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{BA16}\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

As
\Delta\textsubscript{i}
is increasing or static when
n\textsubscript{i}
increases up till
\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\;(\bar{\epsilon}=0)
we get:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1)

If
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq n\textsubscript{i}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
then we get that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}
is greater than
\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
and:

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}\geq

\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}<

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1)

From this follows that:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}))=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

This gives that:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1)

This theorem for this specific case is then given by
lemma

53
.

\bullet

\bar{\epsilon}=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=1
and
\text{$\bar{n}$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
:

From
definition

20
follows that:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

If
n\textsubscript{i}=1
then

\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since:

\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in(1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}]

This implies that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since:

n\textsubscript{i}=1=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

This in turn implies that
\bar{\alpha}<\theta
according to
lemma

52
, i.e. we can disregard this case.

\bullet

\bar{\epsilon}=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=1
and
\text{$\bar{n}$}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
:

From
definition

20
follows that:

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

Note that according to
lemma

63

\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>1
which implies that:

2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1

If
n\textsubscript{i}=1
then
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=1
since:

\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}\geq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1-1=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)

If
1\leq n\textsubscript{i}\leq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
then
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since:

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}\geq

\displaystyle 1=\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1})

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}<

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1)

If
n\textsubscript{i}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
then
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since:

\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}<2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in(1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}]

This gives that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
when
n\textsubscript{i}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
which implies that:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}))=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

Since
\delta\textsubscript{i}\leq\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
when

1\leq n\textsubscript{i}\leq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
, we can conclude that:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}\leq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1)

If
n\textsubscript{i}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
then:

n\textsubscript{i}\geq\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+1=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1)

If
\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}=2
then
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1=1
.

If
\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}>2
then as
\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
we also get that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1
since:

n\textsubscript{i}<2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}<2\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-2)<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-2)

This implies that
when
n\textsubscript{i}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
we get:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1))=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

This gives us the same result as the case where
\bar{\epsilon}
equals zero.

\bullet

\bar{\epsilon}\geq 1
and
\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>1
if
\bar{\epsilon}=1
:

From
definition

20
follows that:

\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<(\bar{\epsilon}+1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1

Note that:

\displaystyle(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\begin{aligned} =&\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\geq 1\text{ if }\bar{\epsilon}=1\\
\geq&\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}>1\text{ if }\bar{\epsilon}\geq 2\\
\end{aligned}

If
n\textsubscript{i}\leq(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
then
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=1
since:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}\geq

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1-((\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)

If
(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}\leq n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
then
\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\bar{\epsilon}\leq\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since:

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}\geq(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\bar{\epsilon})

\displaystyle n\textsubscript{i}<\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-\bar{\epsilon})<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-\bar{\epsilon})

If
n\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1\text{ then }\bar{\delta}\textsubscript{i}>\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}
since:

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}-n\textsubscript{i}<

\displaystyle(\bar{\epsilon}+1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1-(\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}\textsuperscript{s}(1)<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in(1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}]

This gives that
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\bar{\epsilon}
when
n\textsubscript{i}=\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
which in turn implies that:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\bar{\epsilon}))=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

Since
\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}+1-\bar{\epsilon}>1
according to
lemma

66
we can conclude that:

\underset{n\textsubscript{i}\leq\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1}{max}\;a\textsubscript{3}n\textsubscript{i}+a\textsubscript{2}(\Delta\textsubscript{i}-1)=a\textsubscript{3}(\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+(\bar{\epsilon}-1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})-1)

If
\text{$\bar{n}$}=\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}-1
we get
g(A)
from this max. If
\bar{n}
is greater than this then for
n\textsubscript{i}
greater than this we get:

n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-\bar{\epsilon})

If
\bar{\epsilon}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1
then
\delta\textsubscript{i}=\dot{\delta}\textsubscript{i}=\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-\bar{\epsilon}=1
. Else we get the same result since:

n\textsubscript{i}\leq\text{$\bar{n}$}<(\bar{\epsilon}+1)\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}=h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1-\bar{\epsilon})<h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\delta)\;\forall\;\delta\in[1,\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-1-\bar{\epsilon})

This implies that for
n\textsubscript{i}\geq\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}
we get:

\Delta\textsubscript{i}=h(h\textsubscript{$\psi$}(\hat{\delta}\textsubscript{1}-\bar{\epsilon}))=h(\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi+1$}+\bar{\epsilon}\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi+1$})

This theorem for this specific case is then proven in a manner equivalent to the proof for the cases above.
∎

6
Examples

In this section, we show how to compute the Frobenius number by means of the algorithm presented here for various examples. (The entire algorithm is presented in the introduction.) However, no example is provided for the cases where:

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
,
\bar{\alpha}>\theta
,
\text{$\bar{n}$}<\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}-1
and
2\bar{\varphi}\textsubscript{$\psi$}>\varphi\textsubscript{$\psi$}

The reason for this is that the author has not been able to find any set
A
corresponding to this case.

Example 1
.

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}

\displaystyle\text{Given }A=\{9,11,20\}

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{0}=mod(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},\;a\textsubscript{1})=8

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}=108>a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}=99

\displaystyle g(A)=a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{2}-a\textsubscript{1}=79

Example 2
.

a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}
,
\bar{\alpha}<\theta
and
2a\textsubscript{0}<a\textsubscript{1}

\displaystyle\text{Given }A=\{53,55,82\}

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{0}=mod(-a\textsubscript{2}\textsuperscript{-1}a\textsubscript{3},\;a\textsubscript{1})=12

\displaystyle a\textsubscript{3}+a\textsubscript{2}a\textsubscript{0}=742<a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}=2915

\displaystyle 2a\textsubscript{0}=24<a\textsubscript{1}=53

\displaystyle\alpha=a\textsubscript{0}=12

\displaystyle\beta=a\textsubscript{2}\alpha+a\textsubscript{3}=742

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}=mod(a\textsubscript{1},\;\alpha)=5<\theta=\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{3}}{\beta}=5.857

\displaystyle\text{$\bar{n}$}=\biggl\lceil\frac{a\textsubscript{1}a\textsubscript{2}}{\beta}\biggl\rceil-1=3

\displaystyle g(A)=max(\begin{aligned} &a\textsubscript{3}\text{$\bar{n}$}+a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{0}-1),\\
&a\textsubscript{2}(a\textsubscript{1}-\text{$\bar{n}$}a\textsubscript{0}-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=max(851,880)-53=827

then
g(A)
is given by

.

.

&a\textsubscript{3}(\text{$\bar{n}$}-1)+a\textsubscript{2}(\alpha-1))-a\textsubscript{1}\end{aligned}

7
Time complexity analysis

In this section, we will show that the worst case time complexity of the algorithm presented here is
O(\log a\textsubscript{2})
by comparing its time complexity with the time complexity of the Euclidean algorithm with positive remainders. (From now on we will simply write time complexity and let it be implied that it refers to the worst case.) The Euclidean algorithm finds the greatest common divisor of two integers. If these integers are
a
and
b
, then according to Donald E. Knuth
[
6
]
, the time complexity of the Euclidean algorithm is
O(\log(min(a,b)))
.

The Frobenius number of a set
A
, consisting of three arbitrary positive integers, can with the help of Johnson’s theorem (see
theorem

2
) be computed by means of a closed form formula from the Frobenius number of a set
A^{\prime}
, consisting of pairwise coprimes. Computing closed form formulas generally takes constant time. The algorithm presented here assumes that we are starting with a set such as
A^{\prime}
. To get this set from the arbitrary set
A
, we need to find the greatest common divisor of each pair in
A
, i.e. the time complexity of creating
A^{\prime}
from
A
is
O(log(a\textsubscript{2}))
assuming that
a\textsubscript{2}
is the second largest integer in this set.

When we have transformed the arbitrary set
A
into a set such as
A^{\prime}
,
a\textsubscript{0}
(see
definition

9
) is computed based on this set. Therefore, we must
compute the multiplicative inverse of
a\textsubscript{2}
modulo
a\textsubscript{1}
. This can be done by means of the extended Euclidean algorithm. The time complexity for finding the multiplicative inverse of an integer
a
modulo
b
by means of this algorithm is the same as finding the greatest common divisor of these integers by means of the ordinary Euclidean algorithm, i.e. the time complexity for computing
a\textsubscript{0}
is
O(log(a\textsubscript{1}))
. In some cases we also have to compute
a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1}
, which takes less time since
a\textsubscript{0}
is less than
a\textsubscript{1}
.

In some cases, we then have to compute the diff-mod sequences starting with
(\bar{\alpha},\alpha)
and
(a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1},a\textsubscript{1})
respectively. In
section

2
we show that the time complexity for computing a diff-mod sequence starting with the pair
(a,b)
is
O(log(a))
. Since
\bar{\alpha}
and
a\textsubscript{0}\textsuperscript{-1}
are both less than
a\textsubscript{1}
, the time complexity for computing the diff-mod sequences we need, is less than
O(log(a\textsubscript{1}))
. Once these diff-mod sequences have been computed, the Frobenius number is given by means of closed form formulas based on specific pairs in these sequences.

To sum up this complexity analysis, we can conclude that the algorithm presented here consists of a constant number of steps, which time complexity is
O(\log a\textsubscript{2})
or less, implying that the overall time complexity is that as well. Greenberg’s algorithm
[
4
]
is the fastest algorithm known today for solving the three variable case of Frobenius problem and its worst case time complexity is
O(\log a\textsubscript{1})
which one can argue is faster. However, the time complexity of both algorithms is a logarithmic function of
A
.
